T.C. Memo. 2026-113

[*6] MEMORANDUM FINDINGS OF FACT AND OPINION
MORRISON, Judge: In a Notice of Deficiency dated February 6, 2019, respondent, the Commissioner of Internal Revenue, determined the following deficiencies and penalties with respect to the 2008 and 2009 tax returns of petitioner Eric Maurice Pinckney:
Year Deficiency § 6663 Penalty1
2008 $71,581 $53,685.75
2009 75,638 56,728.50
Pinckney timely filed his Petition with this Court on March 13, 2019, seeking review of the deficiency and penalty determinations. We have jurisdiction pursuant to section 6213(a).
We hold:
1. Pinckney had unreported interest income of $3,352 for the 2008 tax year, as determined in the Notice of Deficiency. See infra Part I.A.
2. Pinckney failed to report $463 of gross receipts of Pinckney Business Consulting for the 2008 tax year, as determined in the Notice of Deficiency. See infra Part I.B.
3. Daitech had gross receipts of $376,712 for the 2008 tax year rather than $382,243 as determined in the Notice of Deficiency or $200,500 as reported by Pinckney on this return. See infra Part I.C.
4. The Notice of Deficiency correctly disallowed $4,776 of businessexpense deductions Pinckney claimed on the Schedule C, Profit or Loss From Business, for Pinckney Business Consulting for 2008. See infra Part I.D.
1 Unless otherwise indicated, references to sections are to the Internal Revenue Code of 1986, Title 26 of the United States Code (I.R.C.), in effect at all relevant times; regulation references are to the Code of Federal Regulations, Title 26 (Treas. Reg.), in effect at all relevant times; and Rule references are to the Tax Court Rules of Practice and Procedure. We refer to the respondent as the IRS (the initialism for the Internal Revenue Service).
7
[*7] 5. The Notice of Deficiency correctly determined that Daitech’s cost of goods sold is zero for 2008 rather than $23,231 as reported by Pinckney on his return. See infra Part I.E.
6. The Notice of Deficiency correctly determined that Daitech’s business-expense deductions are $132,444 for 2008, not $141,883 as claimed by Pinckney on his return. See infra Part I.F.
7. The Notice of Deficiency correctly determined that Pinckney’s home-mortgage interest deduction for 2008 is $39,667 rather than $69,981 as claimed on his return. See infra Part I.G.
8. The Notice of Deficiency correctly determined that Pinckney’s charitable-contribution deduction for 2008 is $4,840 rather than $5,350 as claimed on his return. See infra Part I.H.
9. For 2008, Daitech was a disregarded entity, not an S corporation, and therefore Pinckney’s self-employment income includes income with respect to Daitech’s activities. See infra Part I.I.
10. For 2009, Pinckney failed to report $20 of other income, as determined by the Notice of Deficiency. See infra Part II.A.
11. For 2009, Pinckney failed to report $445 of capital-gain income, as determined by the Notice of Deficiency. See infra Part II.B.
12. For 2009, Pinckney failed to report $292 of dividend income, as determined by the Notice of Deficiency. See infra Part II.C.
13. For 2009, Daitech had gross receipts and other income of $340,765, as determined by the Notice of Deficiency, rather than $143,080 as reported on Pinckney’s return. See infra Part II.D.
14. The Notice of Deficiency correctly determined that Daitech’s cost of goods sold is zero for 2009, not $30,667 as reported by Pinckney on the Schedule C for Daitech. See infra Part II.E.
15. Daitech had business-expenses deductions of $66,963 for 2009, rather than $63,199 as determined in the Notice of Deficiency or $60,202 as claimed on the Schedule C. See infra Part II.F.
16. The Notice of Deficiency was correct to disallow a $5,000 deduction for self-employed health insurance claimed by Pinckney on his return for 2009. See infra Part II.G.
8
[*8] 17. The Notice of Deficiency did not err in determining that Pinckney’s charitable-contribution deduction is $3,850 for 2009, not $4,350 as claimed on his return. See infra Part II.H.
18. For 2009, Daitech was a disregarded entity rather than an S corporation, and therefore Pinckney’s self-employment income includes income with respect to Daitech’s activities. See infra Part II.I.
19. Pinckney is liable for penalties under section 6663(a) for 2008 and 2009. See infra Part III.
20. The periods of limitation for assessment as to tax years 2008 and 2009 did not expire before the IRS mailed the Notice of Deficiency. See infra Part IV.
21. The Court does not have jurisdiction in this case to determine that the restitution-based assessments violated section 6201(a)(4)(B). See infra Part V.
22. Myers did not violate Pinckney’s Fourth Amendment rights by misrepresenting the nature of the examination. See infra Part VI.
23. The Court did not commit prejudicial errors during the trial. See infra Part VII.
FINDINGS OF FACT
Pinckney resided in South Carolina when he filed his Petition.2
Pinckney owned and operated Daitech Tax Services, LLC.
Pinckney has a master’s degree in computer information systems from Webster University.
On November 22, 2002, Pinckney organized Daitech Tax Services as a limited liability company (LLC) in the State of South Carolina.
2 Section 7482(b) governs the venue for appeal from a decision of this Court. Unless the parties stipulate to the contrary, the appellate venue is the circuit in which the individual petitioner resided when the petition was filed. § 7482(b)(1)(A). South Carolina is in the Fourth Circuit. 28 U.S.C. § 41. Under the rule laid down in Golsen v. Commissioner, 54 T.C. 742, 756–57 (1970), aff’d, 445 F.2d 985 (10th Cir. 1971), we abide by Fourth Circuit precedent squarely on point in deciding this case.
9
[*9] Pinckney has always been the sole member of Daitech. Daitech was in the business of preparing and filing tax returns.
By 2008, Daitech had adopted a practice of preparing federal income tax returns for its customers that produced modest refunds through the manipulation of a refundable credit called the earned income credit. The amount of this credit varies with the taxpayer’s earned income (defined as wages and self-employment income). § 32(b)(2), (c)(2)(A). As earned income increases, the amount of the credit increases as well. § 32(a)(1). But after a point, an increase in earned income results in a decrease in the credit. § 32(a)(2)(B). If a customer’s actual earned income was too low, Pinckney would inflate the customer’s business income on the customer’s Schedule C to increase the reported earned income to the amount that would produce the peak refund. If a customer’s actual earned income was too high, Pinckney would underreport gross income, or fabricate business-expense deductions, on the customer’s Schedule C to push the reported earned income down to the amount that would produce the peak refund. One method by which Pinckney would underreport gross income on the Schedule C would be to falsely claim that a Daitech customer’s business income should be reduced by cost of goods sold.
Daitech received fees when its customers received their refunds.
During 2008 and 2009, Lemuel Brown was employed as the office manager of Daitech.
During 2008 and 2009, Brandon Samuels was employed as the tax account manager of Daitech.
During 2008 and 2009, Pinckney owned a house on 155 Dunleith Way, Irmo, South Carolina, which was his principal residence.
Daitech’s main office was at 2005 Hampton Street, Columbia, South Carolina. Daitech also had an office at 4430 Broad River Road, Columbia, South Carolina. Daitech may have had other offices, but the record does not indicate whether these offices were in operation during 2008 and 2009.
Daitech’s offices were rented rather than owned. Pinckney’s 2008 return suggests, through its claim of deductions for the “business use of home” on the Schedule C for Daitech, that Pinckney’s house in Irmo was used by Daitech in Daitech’s operations. However, there is insufficient evidence to support a finding that Daitech work was performed at his
10
[*10] house, and thus, we find that Daitech work was not performed at his house.
One of Pinckney’s accounts was the Daitech Account.
In 2008 and 2009, Pinckney owned a bank account at First Community Bank in Daitech’s name with an account number ending in 6122 (the “Daitech Account”). The trial record contains records from the Daitech Account, consisting of bank statements, copies of checks written on the account, “Cash In Tickets” (documents prepared by the bank teller each time an account holder deposited cash, showing the date and amount of the deposit), and deposit slips (documents prepared by an account holder each time an account holder made a deposit of cash or checks, showing the date of the deposit, the total amount of checks deposited, and the amount of any cash deposited). These records are limited to 2008 and 2009.
The bank records for the Daitech Account indicate that Pinckney had other financial accounts. Taking 2008, for example, Pinckney made payments to financial accounts at Bank of America and E*Trade Bank from the Daitech Account. And during 2008, the Daitech Account received deposits from an account at First National Bank of Omaha with an account number ending in 4276, and from an account with American Funds Investment, both of which Pinckney owned. Furthermore, during 2008 Pinckney made payments from the Daitech Account to the following creditors (including for credit cards): Bank of America, First National Bank of Omaha for an account ending in 0467, Discover, IndyMac Bank, Sallie Mae Educational Loan, and SunTrust. There are no account statements in the trial record for any financial accounts or lending relationships other than for the Daitech Account.
For the 2008 tax year, One West Bank FSB issued a Form 1099– INT, Interest Income, to Pinckney stating it had paid him $3,326 of interest. It sent a copy of the form to the IRS. The payments were not deposited into the Daitech Account.
For the 2008 tax year, Carolina Collegiate Federal Credit Union issued a Form 1099–INT to Pinckney stating it had paid him $26 of interest. It sent a copy of the form to the IRS. The payments were not deposited into the Daitech Account.
For the 2008 tax year, Daitech issued a Form 1099–MISC, Miscellaneous Income, to Pinckney, stating that it had paid $19,900 in nonemployee compensation. Pinckney reported this amount as $9,900
11
[*11] on an unsigned Form 1040–X, Amended U.S. Individual Income Tax Return, for the 2008 tax year. The payments were not deposited into the Daitech Account.
For the 2008 tax year, Indymac Federal Bank Home Servicing issued Pinckney a Form 1098, Mortgage Interest Statement, stating that he had paid mortgage interest of $36,362.3
For the 2009 tax year, E*Trade Clearing, LLC, issued a Form 1099–MISC to Pinckney stating it had paid him $20 in income. It sent a copy of the form to the IRS. The payment was not deposited into the Daitech Account.
For the 2009 tax year, E*Trade Clearing, LLC, issued a Form 1099–B, Proceeds From Broker and Barter Exchange Transactions, to Pinckney stating he had received capital gain income of $445. It sent a copy of the form to the IRS. The payments were not deposited into the Daitech Account.
For the 2009 tax year, E*Trade Clearing, LLC, issued a Form 1099–DIV, Dividends and Distributions, stating he had received dividend income of $292. It sent a copy of the form to the IRS. The payments were not deposited into the Daitech Account.
On May 12, 2009, Pinckney filed his 2008 return, which included Schedule Cs, Profit and Loss From Business, for both Daitech and Pinckney Business Consulting.
On April 13, 2009, the due date for Pinckney to file his 2008 return was extended to October 15, 2009.
On May 12, 2009, Pinckney filed his federal income tax return on Form 1040, U.S. Individual Income Tax Return, for the 2008 tax year. He reported the following:

The return did not include a copy of the Form 1099–INT from One West Bank FSB, the Form 1099–INT from Carolina Collegiate Federal Credit Union, or the Form 1099–MISC from Daitech.

4 Pinckney miscalculated the amount owed as $3,055. Pinckney reported total tax of $5,903 and total payments of $2,892. The amount owed is the difference between total tax and total payments. The total tax ($5,903) minus the total payments ($2,892) equals $3,011, not $3,055.

Pinckney reported on the Schedule SE, Self-Employment Tax, to his Form 1040 that his income from self-employment was $41,773, which was the sum of the $35,386 profit he reported for Daitech and the $6,387 profit he reported for Pinckney Business Consulting.
The Form 1040 stated that it was prepared by Brown. This may have been true. However, the return was filed and approved by Pinckney.
On April 6, 2010, Pinckney filed his 2009 return, which included a Schedule C for Daitech but not Pinckney Business Consulting.
On April 6, 2010, Pinckney filed his federal income tax return for his 2009 tax year. The due date for filing the return had been extended to October 15, 2010. On his Form 1040, Pinckney reported the following:

The return did not include the Form 1099–INT from E*Trade Clearing, LLC, the Form 1099–B from E*Trade Clearing, LLC, the Form 1099–DIV from E*Trade Clearing, LLC, nor the Form 1099–MISC from E*Trade Clearing, LLC.

16
[*16] Pinckney reported the following on the Schedule C for Daitech:
Item Amount Income
Gross receipts or sales $5,530 Cost of goods sold 30,667 Gross profit $(25,137) Other income 137,550 Gross income 112,413 Expenses
Advertising 5,250 Car and truck expenses 7,675 Contract labor 3,452 Office expense 7,250 Other business property 15,600 Taxes and licenses 365 Travel 450 Deductible meals and entertainment 5,460 Utilities 11,450 Other expenses 3,250 Total expenses $60,202 Net profit $52,211
The Schedule C for Daitech did not claim any deduction for the expenses of the business use of a home. It reported that the $30,667 costof-goods-sold amount was the sum of $25,417 in cost of labor and $5,250 for materials and supplies.
Pinckney did not include a Schedule C for Pinckney Business Consulting with his 2009 return.
Pinckney reported on the Schedule SE to his Form 1040 that his income from self-employment was $52,211, which was the profit he reported for Daitech.
The Form 1040 stated that it was prepared by Brown. This may have been true. However, the return was filed by Pinckney and approved by Pinckney.
The IRS began a civil examination of Pinckney’s 2008 and 2009 returns, issued him a 30-day letter, and then suspended the examination.
Pinckney’s 2008 tax return was selected by the IRS for civil examination. The return was selected pursuant to a random audit.
On August 6, 2010, the IRS assigned Tax Compliance Officer Carlton Myers to the examination.
17
[*17] On September 21, 2010, Myers mailed Pinckney an initial contact letter notifying him that his 2008 tax return had been selected for examination and asking Pinckney to contact Myers within 10 days to set up an interview. The letter stated that the examination would be primarily focused on the car-and-truck expenses reported on the Schedule C for Pinckney Business Consulting, the cost of goods sold reported on the Schedule C for Daitech, and the Schedule A homemortgage-interest deduction.
On September 30, 2010, Myers interviewed Pinckney at an IRS office in Columbia, South Carolina.
On February 11, 2011, Myers expanded the examination to include Pinckney’s 2009 tax year.
During the examination, Pinckney provided Myers with some receipts to substantiate his claimed Schedule A deductions and claimed Schedule C business-expense deductions for 2008 and 2009. As trial testimony suggests, some of the receipts were illegible and none were offered as evidence at trial. Testimony about the receipts was vague, and thus very little is known about these receipts.
During the examination, Pinckney also provided Myers with documents to show that the gross receipts of Pinckney Business Consulting for 2008 were $16,520. These documents were (1) a one-page computer printout of deposits totaling $10,363, which was admitted as Exhibit 36-J; (2) a Form 1099–MISC from HSBC Taxpayer Financial Services, Inc., showing $1,117 of payments to Pinckney, which was admitted as Exhibit 35-J; and (3) a Form 1099–MISC from HSBC Taxpayer Financial Services, Inc., showing $5,040 of payments to Pinckney, which was admitted as Exhibit 33-J.
On January 19, 2012, Myers issued a 30-day letter to Pinckney that stated adjustments to Pinckney’s 2008 and 2009 returns. This 30day letter will be referred to as the 2012 30-day letter. A second 30-day letter was issued on May 18, 2018. This 30-day letter will be referred to as the 2018 30-day letter. In discussing the 2012 30-day letter and the 2018 30-day letter, we will often compare these letters to the Notice of Deficiency. The Notice of Deficiency will sometimes be referred to as the 90-day letter.

24
[*24] As stated above, the 2012 30-day letter asserted that Pinckney had a deficiency of $15,655 for 2009. The 2012 30-day letter also asserted that Pinckney owed a 20% section 6662 accuracy-related penalty of $3,131. The letter stated that if Pinckney agreed with the letter, he had 30 days to sign the letter and return it to the IRS; and that if he did not agree with it, he had 30 days to request a conference with the IRS Office of Appeals.
Pinckney did not agree with the 2012 30-day letter. However, there is no indication in the record that he requested a conference with Appeals in response.
On January 20, 2012, Pinckney consented to extend the expiration date of the usual three-year period for assessing his 2008 tax liability to June 30, 2013. However, Pinckney did not execute a similar consent for tax year 2009.
On or after April 8, 2012, Pinckney submitted to the IRS a Form 1120–S, U.S. Income Tax Return for an S Corporation, for Daitech for 2008.5 The Form 1120–S stated that Daitech was a subchapter S corporation with ordinary business income of $93,597, which was computed with an $18,807 deduction for salaries and wages paid to unnamed employees. The Form 1120–S stated that the effective date of Daitech’s S election was January 1, 2002. The 2008 Form 1120–S is Exhibit 25-P.
On or after April 8, 2012, Pinckney submitted to the IRS a Form 1040–X for 2008.6 The amended return stated that Pinckney earned wages of $18,807 and that he had passthrough income from Daitech of $93,597. The explanation for the amendment was: “DAITECH TAX SERVICES LLC IS A [sic] S-CORP SO A [sic] 1120-S FORM WAS FILED[.] AMENDED TAX RETURN TO REMOVE SCHEDULE C FOR DAITECH TAX SERVICES. ADDED SCH E NON PASSIVE [sic] INCOME FROM SCH K-1 OF THE 1120-S RETURN FOR DAITECH TAX SERVICES[.] UPDATED 8829 FORM[.]” The self-employment income calculated on the amended return consisted of $3,199 of net
5 Pinckney contends that the return was submitted “[o]n or around April 8, 2012.” The IRS contends that the return was submitted “approximately . . . , July 26, 2021.” We need not, and do not, resolve the controversy about the date of submission.
6 Pinckney contends that the amended return was submitted “[o]n or around April 8, 2012.” The IRS contends that the return was submitted “approximately . . . , February 27, 2020.” We need not, and do not, resolve the controversy about the date of submission.
25
[*25] profit stated to be the income of Pinckney Business Consulting. It did not include the passthrough income from Daitech. The total tax liability reported was $3,752. The Form 1040–X was admitted as Exhibit 23-P.
On or about April 8, 2012, Pinckney submitted to the IRS a Form 1120–S for Daitech for 2009.7 The Form 1120–S stated that Daitech was a subchapter S corporation with ordinary business income of $39,316, which was computed with a $51,237 deduction for salaries and wages paid to unnamed employees. The Form 1120–S stated that the effective date of Daitech’s S election was January 1, 2002. The 2009 Form 1120– S was admitted as Exhibit 26-P.
On or about April 8, 2012, Pinckney submitted to the IRS a Form 1040–X for 2009,8 admitted as Exhibit 24-P. The amended return reported that Pinckney earned wages of $26,100 and that he had passthrough income from Daitech of $39,316. The explanation for the amendment was: “AMENDED RETURN TO REMOVE SCH C. DAITECH TAX SERVICES IS AN LLC ELECTED AS [AN] S-CORP. ALL EXPENSES HAS [sic] BEEN MOVED TO 1120-S AND FILED ON BEHALF OF DAITECH TAX. ATTACHED IS A COPY OF THE 2553 ELECTION. MOVED 1099 MISC INCOME FROM DAITECH TAX SERVICES TO SCH C PINCKNEY BUSINESS CONSULTING.[ ]ATTACHED IS A COPY OF 1120-S[.]” Contrary to this explanation, neither a Form 2553, Election By a Small Business Corporation, nor a Form 1120–S was attached to the amended return. The self-employment income calculated on the amended return consisted of $5,530 of net profit stated to be the income of Pinckney Business Consulting. It did not include the passthrough income from Daitech. The total tax liability reported was $3,202.
The civil examination was suspended; criminal proceedings were conducted, during which the usual three-year periods for assessing tax for tax years 2008 and 2009 expired.
On April 11, 2012, Myers spoke with IRS Special Agent Robert Key of the IRS Criminal Investigation Division. During this
7 Both Pinckney and the IRS agree that the return was submitted “[o]n or around April 8, 2012.”
8 Both Pinckney and the IRS agree that the return was submitted “[o]n or around April 8, 2012.”
26
[*26] conversation, Key told Myers to suspend the civil examination. This was the sole conversation Myers ever had with Key.
That same day, April 11, 2012, Key followed up with an email to Myers. In the email, Key stated that “[a]fter speaking with my manager, it’s imperative that no further action or contact is made on the Pinckney case. Therefore, please hold off on closing the case until notified. . . . I’ll be looking for your case in the mail next week.” Thereafter, the IRS’s civil examination of Pinckney was suspended.
On May 7, 2012, Sarah Norris, Myers’s supervisor, emailed Key. In the email, Sarah asked: “Are there any new developments in the Pinckney/Daitech case? We are still holding in suspense.”
That same day, May 7, 2012, Key responded to Norris by email: “Mr. Pinckney is still under criminal investigation; however, we are still in a covert investigative status. Therefore, please continue to hold Pinckney’s civil case in suspense and cease all contact with Pinckney until we have concluded our investigation. I’ll make sure to let you know if the opportunity arrives for you to release the civil case from suspense prior to the conclusion of the criminal investigation.” Norris then forwarded Key’s response to Myers.
On August 20, 2014, a grand jury issued Pinckney an indictment in the criminal case of United States v. Pinckney, No. 3:14-CR-606-JMC, 2015 WL 13657243 (D.S.C. July 22, 2015), followed by a superseding indictment on July 22, 2015. The superseding indictment charged Pinckney with 12 counts:
Count 1 alleged that Pinckney, Brown, Samuels, and other
persons conspired to submit false income tax returns for customers of Daitech in violation of 18 U.S.C. § 371. The indictment charged that Pinckney, Brown, and Samuels engaged in the following overt acts in furtherance of the conspiracy: (1) operation of Daitech by Pinckney; (2) soliciting and providing tax preparation services for Daitech customers; (3) falsifying Schedules A for Daitech customers; (4) falsifying Schedules C for Daitech customers; (5) falsifying claims for child and dependent care credits and education credits for Daitech customers; (6) filing false and fraudulent tax returns for Daitech customers; (7) causing the IRS to issue fraudulent tax refunds to Daitech customers; and (8) performing the acts alleged in Counts 5 through 12.
27
[*27]
Count 2 alleged that in violation of I.R.C. § 7206(1) Pinckney
submitted a false Form 1040 for himself for tax year 2008, on which he reported that his taxable income was $0, though his actual taxable income was at least $180,362.77, and on which he reported his tax liability was $5,903, though his actual tax liability was at least $53,601.38.
Count 3 alleged that in violation of I.R.C. § 7206(1) Pinckney
submitted a false Form 1040 for himself for tax year 2009 on which he reported that his taxable income was $22,998, though his actual taxable income was at least $340,765.25, and on which he reported his tax liability was $10,406, though his actual tax liability was at least $117,957.89.
Count 4 alleged that in violation of I.R.C. § 7206(1) Pinckney
submitted a false Form 1040 for himself for tax year 2010 on which he reported that his taxable income was $22,239, though his actual taxable income was at least $239,514.27, and on which he reported that his tax liability was $9,482, though his actual tax liability was at least $81,184.76.
Count 5 alleged that in violation of I.R.C. § 7206(2) Pinckney
filed a Form 1040 for Daitech customer Bernard F. for tax year 2008, falsely claiming a $19,396 deduction for footman cleaning.
Count 6 alleged that in violation of I.R.C. § 7206(2) Pinckney
filed a Form 1040 for Daitech customer Jarvis R. for tax year 2009, falsely claiming a $10,820 deduction for vehicle expenses.
Count 7 alleged that in violation of I.R.C. § 7206(2) Pinckney
filed a Form 1040 for Daitech customer Shelton B. for tax year 2010, falsely claiming a $4,076 deduction for unreimbursed employee expenses.
Count 8 alleged that in violation of I.R.C. § 7206(2) Pinckney
filed a Form 1040 for tax year 2011 for Daitech customer Chauncey D., falsely claiming a $3,273 deduction for gifts to charity.
Count 9 alleged that in violation of I.R.C. § 7206(2) Brown filed
a Form 1040 for tax year 2008 for Daitech customer Rodney
28
[*28]
B., falsely claiming a $19,093 deduction for unreimbursed employee expenses.
Count 10 alleged that in violation of I.R.C. § 7206(2) Brown
filed a Form 1040 for tax year 2009 for Daitech customer Bernard F., falsely claiming a $7,315 deduction for mortgage interest.
Count 11 alleged that in violation of I.R.C. § 7206(2) Brown
filed a Form 1040 for tax year 2010 for Daitech customer Umar M., falsely claiming a $2,604 deduction for unreimbursed employee expenses.
Count 12 alleged that in violation of I.R.C. § 7206(2) Brown
filed a Form 1040 for tax year 2011 for Daitech customer Bernard F., falsely claiming a $5,963 deduction for mortgage interest.
On November 18, 2014, the IRS made an entry in an IRS record called an “Account Transcript” for the tax year 2008. The entry stated that the IRS could assess Pinckney’s tax for tax year 2008 until May 12, 2015. This date was six years after Pinckney’s tax year 2008 return was filed.
Then, on March 4, 2015, the IRS made an entry on Pinckney’s Account Transcript for tax year 2009. The entry stated that the IRS could assess Pinckney’s tax for tax year 2009 until September 24, 2013. This date was three years after the date the IRS recorded Pinckney’s tax year 2009 return as received.9
Then, on July 31, 2015, Pinckney entered into a plea agreement in which he pleaded guilty to count 1 of the superseding indictment, i.e., entering into a criminal conspiracy in violation of 18 U.S.C. § 371. Paragraph 2 of the plea agreement provides:
Defendant [Pinckney] agrees to make full restitution under 18 U.S.C. § 3556 in an amount to be determined by the Court at the time of sentencing . . . .
. . . .
9 Although the trial record shows the return was filed on April 6, 2010, the IRS had recorded that it received the return on September 24, 2010.
29
[*29]
If the Court orders the Defendant to pay restitution to the IRS for the failure to pay tax, either directly as part of the sentence or as a condition of supervised release, the IRS will use the restitution order as the basis for a civil assessment. See 26 U.S.C. § 6201(a)(4). The Defendant does not have the right to challenge the amount of this assessment. See 26 U.S.C. § 6201(a)(4)(C). Neither the existence of a restitution payment schedule nor the Defendant’s timely payment of restitution according to that schedule will preclude the IRS from administrative collection of the restitution-based assessment, including levy and distraint order under 26 U.S.C. § 6331.
Paragraph 3 of the plea agreement provides:
Defendant agrees not to file any claim for refund of taxes or interest represented by any amount of restitution paid pursuant to this agreement.
The parties [Pinckney and the United States government] understand that Defendant will receive proper credit, consistent with paragraph 2 above, [in which Pinckney agrees to make a full restitution,] for the payments made pursuant to this agreement. Except as set forth in the previous sentence, nothing in this agreement shall limit the IRS in its lawful examination, determination, assessment, or collection of any taxes, penalties, or interest due from the Defendant for the time periods(s) covered by this agreement or any other time period.
Defendant agrees that this agreement, or any judgment, order, release or satisfaction issued in connection with this agreement, will not satisfy, settle or compromise the Defendant’s obligation to pay the balance of any remaining civil liabilities, including tax, additional tax, additions to tax, interest and penalties, owed to the IRS for the time periods(s) covered by this agreement or any other time period.
Paragraph 6 of the plea agreement, which relates to Pinckney’s cooperation, provides:
The Defendant agrees to be fully truthful and forthright with federal, state and local law enforcement agencies by
30
[*30]
providing full, complete and truthful information about all criminal activities about which he has knowledge. The Defendant must provide full, complete and truthful debriefings about these unlawful activities and must fully disclose and provide truthful information to the Government including any books, papers, or documents or any other items of evidentiary value to the investigation. The Defendant must also testify fully and truthfully before any grand juries and at any trials or other proceedings if called upon to do so by the Government, subject to prosecution for perjury for not testifying truthfully. The failure of the Defendant to be fully truthful and forthright at any stage will, at the sole election of the Government, cause the obligations of the Government within this [plea agreement] to become null and void due to the lack of truthfulness on the part of the Defendant.
On March 1, 2016, Pinckney testified for the government in the criminal jury trial of co-conspirator Brandon Samuels, United States v. Samuels, No. 3:14-CR-606-JMC (D.S.C. Aug. 20, 2014), in what was part of the overarching criminal prosecution, see United States v. Samuels, 702 F. App’x 161, 162 (4th Cir. 2017). Pinckney testified in exchange for the dismissal of 11 of the 12 counts against him, and in the hope that he would be sentenced leniently on the count to which he pleaded guilty. When Samuels’ attorney cross-examined Pinckney, Pinckney made several admissions regarding his 2008 and 2009 tax returns. We discuss two of these admissions below:
As to tax year 2008, Pinckney agreed that he “evaded taxes at
least to the sum of $53,601”; he “lied” to the IRS “throughout” his tax return; he “wanted to avoid paying the government” the amount of $53,601; and he reported that his taxable income was zero even though he “knew that was absolutely a lie.”
As to tax year 2009, Pinckney agreed that he “lied on that tax
return” too; he “didn’t want to pay the government $117,957.89,” so he reported that he owed $10,406 in tax on the 2009 return even though that was a “lie”; his 2008 return contained a similar “lie”; and his true income for 2009 was $340,765.25.
31
[*31] On December 1, 2016, the U.S. District Court for the District of South Carolina entered judgment in Pinckney’s criminal case. The district court judged him guilty of count 1 of the superseding indictment and sentenced him to prison for six months. The district court also imposed a monetary penalty on him in two components: (1) a $100 assessment, and (2) restitution in the sum of $334,738.02, to be paid to the IRS in equal monthly installments of $100, commencing 60 days after his release from prison.
The IRS resumed the civil examination, issued a 2018 30-day letter, heard Pinckney’s administrative appeal, and issued the Notice of Deficiency.
On February 3, 2017, an employee of the IRS, Carolyn Heath, wrote a memorandum to the Examination Division charged with Pinckney’s examination. The memorandum stated that the grand jury investigation and prosecution of Pinckney “has been completed and the case is being returned to your group for civil settlement.” The memorandum stated: “The case must not be assigned to any examiner that assisted in the grand jury investigation, as this examiner has become ‘tainted’ and the evidence gathered during a grand jury investigation is protected under the secrecy provisions of Rule 6(e) of the Federal Rule [sic] of Criminal Procedure.”
Soon after February 3, 2017, Myers resumed the civil examination of Pinckney’s returns.
During the resumed examination, Pinckney supplied the IRS with a monthly statement from Indymac Federal Bank Home Servicing that, the IRS later conceded, demonstrated that for 2008 Pinckney paid $3,305 of home mortgage interest in addition to the $36,362 of home mortgage interest shown on the 2008 Form 1098.
On May 11, 2018, Myers wrote an internal memorandum concluding that clear and convincing evidence existed that for tax years 2008 and 2009 there were underpayments, portions of which were due to fraud. Among the evidence cited was Pinckney’s indictment and guilty plea.10
10 Although we admitted portions of the May 11, 2018, internal memorandum, including the material discussed in the text of the Opinion above, we do not rely on the memorandum in determining that Pinckney’s 2008 and 2009 returns were fraudulent.
32
[*32] On May 11, 2018, Myers also issued a second 30-day letter to Pinckney. As explained supra, we refer to this as the 2018 30-day letter. The adjustments to income are shown in the tables produced in this Opinion. See supra pp. 18–23. As can be seen from those tables, the adjustments to income in the 2018 30-day letter are different in many respects from the adjustments to income in the 2012 30-day letter.
The 2018 30-day letter stated that Daitech’s tax year 2008 gross receipts were $382,243 and that this amount was the sum of (1) $201,880, “[p]er information provided by taxpayer” and (2) $180,362.77, “[p]er [the] indictment.” Both of these amounts have appeared in the record before: Recall that the 2012 30-day letter had stated that Daitech’s gross receipts were $201,880, a $1,380 increase compared with the $200,500 reported on the return. Recall, too, that the superseding indictment had stated that Pinckney’s taxable income was at least $180,362.77.
The 2018 30-day letter also stated that Daitech’s tax year 2009 gross receipts were $340,765, and that this amount was “[p]er indictment.” Recall that the superseding indictment had stated that Pinckney’s taxable income was at least $340,765.25.
In addition to making adjustments to Pinckney’s income, which resulted in deficiencies, the 2018 30-day letter also asserted that Pinckney was liable for 75% section 6663 fraud penalties. By contrast, the 2012 30-day letter had asserted that Pinckney was liable for 20% section 6662 penalties. The amounts of the fraud penalties asserted in the 2018 30-day letter were $53,685.75 for 2008 and $56,728.50 for 2009. Myers made the initial determination to assert the fraud penalties and he was identified in the 2018 30-day letter as the IRS point of contact. The 2018 30-day letter was signed by Myers’s immediate supervisor, Norris.
Pinckney disagreed with the IRS’s conclusions in the 2018 30-day letter, and he requested a meeting with the IRS Office of Appeals.
On September 19, 2018, Pinckney met with IRS Appeals Officer Phillip M. Dunn. That same day, Appeals Officer Dunn wrote a letter to Pinckney asking him to review the 2018 30-day letter and identify the issues with which he disagreed, with a deadline of October 4, 2018.
On September 28, 2018, Appeals Officer Dunn wrote a letter to Pinckney regarding the 2008 and 2009 tax years. Dunn wrote: “Related to your records, you will need to contact the US Attorney [sic] Office
33
[*33] (Columbia) . . . and they will put you in contact with the party that has access to your records.”
On February 6, 2019, the IRS Office of Appeals mailed to Pinckney the Notice of Deficiency concerning his 2008 and 2009 tax years. The Notice of Deficiency determined the same adjustments to income, and the same amounts of deficiencies, as the 2018 30-day letter. Whereas the 2018 30-day letter had stated that the $382,243 amount determined to be the gross receipts for Daitech for 2008 was the sum of $201,880 “[p]er information provided by taxpayer” and $180,362.77 “[p]er [the] indictment,” the 90-day letter contained no explanation of the source of the $382,243 amount; and whereas the 2018 30-day letter had stated that the $340,765 amount determined to be the gross receipts of Daitech for 2009 was “[p]er [the] indictment,” the 90-day letter contained no explanation of the source of the $340,765 amount.
Like the 2018 30-day letter, the Notice of Deficiency determined that Pinckney was liable for section 6663 fraud penalties for both years at issue and in the same amounts as stated in the 2018 30-day letter. The Notice of Deficiency determined an underpayment of $71,581 for 2008, and that the full amount of this underpayment was due to fraud. The Notice of Deficiency determined an underpayment of $75,638 for 2009 and that, similarly, the full amount of this underpayment was due to fraud.
On October 15, 2019, the IRS Criminal Investigation Division approved a Form 14104, Notification of Court Ordered Criminal Restitution Payable to IRS. The Form 14104 stated how the IRS would assess the $334,738.02 of restitution payable by Pinckney to the IRS. The Form 14104 stated that the assessment would be allocated among the following tax liabilities: $47,698.38 to Pinckney’s 2008 liability; (2) $107,551.88 to Pinckney’s 2009 liability; (3) $71,702.76 to Pinckney’s 2010 liability; and (4) $107,785 to Pinckney’s client tax liabilities ($21,284 for 2009, $44,464 for 2009, and $42,037 for 2010). By year, the $334,738.02 was divided as follows: $69,182.38 to 2008, $152,197.88 to 2009, $101,532.76 to 2010, and $11,825 to 2011.
On January 15, 2020, the IRS assessed restitution liabilities against Pinckney of $66,032.78 for tax year 2008, $100,241.76 for tax year 2010, and $11,825 for tax year 2011.
On November 23, 2020, the IRS assessed a $145,517.88 restitution liability against Pinckney for tax year 2009.
34
[*34] On May 21, 2021, the IRS filed a notice of federal tax lien regarding assessments made on November 27, 2017, of a $9,438.90 “1040” liability for tax year 2011; on September 18, 2017, of a $2,508.14 “1040” liability for tax year 2016; on September 2, 2019, of a $10,000 “6694B”11 liability for tax year 2009; and on September 9, 2019, of a $10,000 “6694B” liability for tax year 2010. The record does not contain a notice to Pinckney that the IRS filed the notice of federal tax lien. See § 6320(a)(1), (2).
On June 10, 2021, the IRS sent Pinckney a notice that on June 10, 2020, it had filed a notice of federal tax lien to collect various tax liabilities that had been assessed on January 15, 2020. These liabilities were a $66,032.78 “R1040” liability for tax year 2008, a $100,241.76 “R1040” liability for tax year 2010, and an $11,825 “R1040” liability for tax year 2011. The notice stated that Pinckney had until July 19, 2021, to request a section 6320 collection-due-process hearing with the IRS Office of Appeals.
On June 28, 2021, the IRS sent Pinckney a notice declaring its intent to levy to collect various tax liabilities. These liabilities included a $66,052.78 “RBA”12 liability for tax year 2008, a $145,517.88 “RBA” liability for tax year 2009, a $100,241.76 “RBA” liability for 2010, and an $11,825 “RBA” liability for tax year 2011. The notice stated that Pinckney had until July 28, 2021, to request a section 6330 collectiondue-process hearing with the IRS Office of Appeals.
On June 29, 2021, the IRS filed a notice of federal tax lien regarding an assessment, made on November 23, 2020, of a $145,517.88 “R1040” liability for tax year 2009. The notice explained that “[p]ursuant to IRC [section] 6201(a)(4), these assessments reflect the amount of restitution ordered payable to the IRS.” The record does not contain a notice to Pinckney that the IRS filed the notice of federal tax lien. See § 6320(a)(1) and (2).
On September 22, 2021, the IRS Office of Appeals sent a letter to Pinckney notifying him it had received his collection-due-process
11 Section 6694(b) imposes a penalty on a tax-return preparer who willfully or recklessly prepares a return that understates tax liability.
12 RBA stands for restitution-based assessment, which is an assessment made under section 6201(a)(4)(A). See Williams v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2025-90, at *3. There is a $20 restitution assessment discrepancy here. The RBA liability on June 8, 2021, was in the amount of $66,052.78, but the June 10, 2021, lien against Pinckney was in the amount of $66,032.78. This opinion utilizes the later amount of $66,032.78.
35
[*35] hearing request regarding a notice of federal tax lien for tax years 2009, 2010, 2011, and 2016. This was seemingly the May 21, 2021, filing of a federal tax lien.
The Court conducted pretrial proceedings, and the IRS acquired the Daitech bank records from First Community Bank.
On October 7, 2019, the Court notified the parties that the trial would take place during a Trial Session that would begin on March 2, 2020.
On December 11, 2019, the Court notified the parties that the trial would instead take place during a Trial Session that would begin on March 9, 2020.
On February 12, 2020, the Court continued the case from the March 9, 2020, Trial Session.
On November 9, 2020, the Court notified the parties that the trial would take place during a Trial Session that would begin on March 15, 2021.
On February 12, 2021, the Court continued the case from the March 15, 2021, Trial Session and set the case for trial during a Trial Session that would begin on January 24, 2022.
On October 25, 2021, the IRS acquired bank records from First Community Bank regarding the Daitech Account. These bank records consist of bank statements for 2008 and 2009, copies of checks written on the account, “Cash In Tickets,” and deposit slips for those two years.
IRS Revenue Agent Lei Baumgartner reviewed the Daitech Account records. She prepared spreadsheets that analyzed all deposits into the Daitech Account and classified each deposit either as taxable or nontaxable. In 2008, there was $572,882.61 in deposits: $380,200 was classified as taxable and $192,682.61 was classified as nontaxable. In 2009, there was $477,590.86 in deposits: $416,171 was classified as taxable and $61,420 was classified as nontaxable. These spreadsheets were admitted at trial as Exhibits 93-J (2008) and 94-J (2009).
On January 3, 2022, the IRS filed its pretrial memorandum. The pretrial memorandum stated that the IRS intended to call several witnesses, including Myers.
36
[*36] On January 19, 2022, the Court held a pretrial hearing. The IRS stated that it still intended to call Myers as a witness. At the pretrial hearing, Pinckney enunciated his theory that the IRS had violated Internal Revenue Manual (IRM) 4565.21(1) and developed a criminal case against him under the guise of a civil investigation.13 The IRS discussed its efforts to serve subpoenas on the witnesses it intended to call and how some of its attempts had been unsuccessful. The Court explained that normally the IRS case is presented first because the IRS has the burden of proving fraud.
The trial occurred.
The trial occurred over five days, from January 24–28, 2022:

On the first day of trial, Monday, January 24, 2022, Pinckney told the Court that he had drafted a subpoena to serve on Myers but that he did not understand the procedure for serving it. The Court explained that Pinckney would need to have the subpoena personally handed to Myers, along with a daily fee for testimony. At the time, the IRS stated that it was unsure whether it would call Myers as a witness. Pinckney
13 IRM 4565.21(1) was part of section 4565 of old IRM 4565, which was quoted in United States v. Powell, 835 F.2d 1095, 1100 n.11 (5th Cir. 1988). The provision in the current IRM that is analogous to old IRM 4565.21(1) is IRM 25.1.3.3(3) (Nov. 17, 2023), which we quote infra Part VI.
37
[*37] reaffirmed that he took the position that the examination was a disguised criminal investigation. It was clear to the parties on the first day of trial that the IRS would be allowed to present its case-in-chief first. Pinckney did not object to this ordering at this time. The first witness the IRS called was Daitech employee Valerie Foster. After the IRS had conducted the direct examination of Foster, Pinckney crossexamined her, followed by the IRS stating on the record that it released her from her subpoena. The IRS’s second witness was Daitech customer Shelton Bacon. After the IRS had conducted the direct examination of Bacon, Pinckney cross-examined him. At the end of his testimony, the IRS stated on the record that it released him from the subpoena.
On the second day of trial, the IRS resumed its case-in-chief. It called another customer of Daitech, Damien White. After White was directly examined by the IRS, Pinckney asked him questions on crossexamination. After White testified, the IRS stated on the record that it released him from the subpoena. The IRS next called a third customer of Daitech as a witness, Jarvis Ross. The IRS asked Ross questions on direct examination. Pinckney then cross-examined Ross, after which the IRS stated on the record that he was released from the subpoena. The IRS then called Pinckney as its last witness of the day. On several occasions during the IRS’s direct examination of Pinckney, the following sequence of events occurred: (1) Pinckney refused to answer a question on the grounds that his answer would incriminate him, (2) the Court overruled Pinckney’s self-incrimination claim and ordered him to answer the question, (3) Pinckney again continued to refuse to answer the question, (4) the IRS made a motion that the Court should make a negative inference from Pinckney’s refusal to answer the question, and (5) the Court reserved ruling on the motion. At the end of the second day, the IRS indicated that it anticipated recalling Pinckney as a witness on the third day of trial to resume his direct testimony. The IRS also explained that, at some point, it would call two more Daitech customers as witnesses and would call Baumgartner, the revenue agent who conducted the bank records analysis. Pinckney then stated that the trial schedule had so far not afforded him enough time to arrange that Myers be served with a subpoena. To afford more time for Pinckney to serve the subpoena, the Court stated that the only witness for the IRS during the third day of trial would be Pinckney himself, that the testimony of the IRS’s remaining witnesses would be delayed until the fourth day, and that Pinckney would not present his case-in-chief until the fifth day of trial. Also at the end of the second day of trial, the IRS asked the Court to prohibit Pinckney from introducing evidence (including cross-examining witnesses) regarding any subject matter for
38
[*38] which he refused to testify. The Court reserved ruling on this motion.
At the beginning of the third day of trial, the Court explained that it still reserved ruling on the IRS’s motion that Pinckney be barred from introducing evidence regarding the subject matter for which he refused to testify. The IRS resumed its direct examination of Pinckney. With respect to some questions, Pinckney again invoked his Fifth Amendment right against self-incrimination, the Court overruled his claim, the Court ordered Pinckney to answer, he refused to answer, and the IRS requested that the Court make a negative inference from his refusal to answer, to which the Court reserved ruling. The IRS concluded its direct examination of Pinckney on the third day of trial.
On the fourth day of trial, Pinckney stated that he had arranged for the Richland County Sheriff’s Office to serve his subpoena on Myers. As its first witness of the day, the IRS called Lisa Ferreira, an IRS employee familiar with the IRS’s handling of criminal restitution. After the IRS conducted the direct examination of Ferreira, Pinckney crossexamined her. The same process occurred for the next three of respondent’s witnesses: Leiron Dawkins, a Daitech customer; Shree Duckett, another customer of Daitech’s; and Lei Baumgartner, the revenue agent who conducted the bank-deposit analysis.14 After Baumgartner finished testifying, Pinckney moved to strike the entire trial record developed so far because the IRS had been allowed to present its case-in-chief first. The Court denied his motion. Pinckney then moved for permission to file an interlocutory appeal of the denial, which was also denied. The IRS then indicated that it rested its case. This meant that Pinckney would begin to present his case-in-chief on the fifth day of trial. Pinckney then indicated that he had received a telephone call from the Richland County Sheriff’s Office and that the office had been unsuccessful in serving his subpoena on Myers.
On the fifth day of trial, Pinckney moved for a continuance in order to again attempt to serve the subpoena on Myers. The Court did not immediately continue the trial and instead ordered Pinckney to call his witnesses other than Myers. Pinckney stated that he wished to call himself as a witness. The IRS objected to Pinckney’s testifying on the grounds that Pinckney had refused to answer questions on direct after
14 Unlike with Foster, Bacon, and Ross, the IRS did not state on the record that these witnesses were released from their subpoenas, although Dawkins and Duckett were presumably under subpoena.
39
[*39] being ordered to do so. The Court overruled the objection in that it allowed Pinckney to testify, although the Court did not resolve the larger question of whether he should be barred from presenting evidence regarding any subject matter for which he had refused to answer questions on direct. Pinckney then proceeded with his direct testimony. He testified that his dispute with the IRS had never been a civil matter and that he “was never placed on notice by Revenue Agent Carlton Myers that the audit w[ould] be for civil, not criminal purposes.” During Pinckney’s testimony on direct, the IRS indicated that it would call Myers as a rebuttal witness. After Pinckney testified on direct, the IRS cross-examined him, and again Pinckney invoked his Fifth Amendment right in response to certain questions. In each such instance, the Court ordered Pinckney to answer, Pinckney still refused to answer, the IRS renewed its motion for the Court to make an adverse inference from Pinckney’s refusal to answer, and the Court reserved ruling on the renewed motion. After Pinckney’s testimony, he attempted to call Myers as a witness. However, Myers had not agreed to testify voluntarily for Pinckney and had not been served a subpoena by Pinckney. Therefore, Myers did not appear as a witness for Pinckney. Pinckney then renewed his request for the Court to continue the case in order to afford time for Pinckney to attempt again to serve a subpoena on Myers. He asked for a continuance to the next week. The Court declined this request. Myers then testified on direct as a rebuttal witness for the IRS. The IRS asked Myers questions related to Pinckney’s allegation that the examination was a disguised criminal investigation. Pinckney then cross-examined Myers, asking him questions related to Pinckney’s allegation that the examination was a disguised criminal investigation. Although the IRS objected to some of Pinckney’s questions during the cross-examination of Myers on the grounds that the questions were outside the scope of the direct examination, the Court sustained none of these objections.
40
[*40] OPINION
I. We sustain all the adjustments underlying the 2008 deficiency determination, except for the adjustment of Daitech’s gross receipts to $382,243, which we determine to instead be $376,712, and except for the adjustment to self-employment tax, the amount of which must account for our finding that Daitech’s gross receipts for 2008 are less than the amount determined in the Notice of Deficiency.
A. Pinckney failed to report $3,352 of interest income.
On his 2008 return, Pinckney did not report any interest income. The Notice of Deficiency determined, however, that he actually earned $3,352 in interest income. We sustain the determination.
The IRS’s determination of a deficiency is usually presumed correct and, as a result, the taxpayer bears the burdens of persuasion and production. Rule 142(a); Foster v. Commissioner, 391 F.2d 727, 735 (4th Cir. 1968) (“[T]he Commissioner’s deficiency determination is presumed correct. The burden is thus on the taxpayer to prove the incorrectness of the deficiency determination.”), aff’g in part, rev’g in part, and remanding on other grounds T.C. Memo. 1965-246; Cozzi v. Commissioner, 88 T.C. 435, 443 (1987). This usual presumption of correctness governs in omitted income cases where the IRS employed a “reasonable method of determining income.” Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d 760, 763–64 (4th Cir. 1993), aff’g T.C. Memo. 1992-153. Some courts have held the IRS’s determination of omitted income is presumed correct only if the IRS establishes a minimal evidentiary foundation linking the taxpayer with the income-producing activity. Anastasato v. Commissioner, 794 F.2d 884, 886–87 (3d Cir. 1986), vacating and remanding T.C. Memo. 1985-101; Weimerskirch v. Commissioner, 596 F.2d 358, 362 (9th Cir. 1979), rev’g 67 T.C. 672 (1977). The U.S. Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit has held that if the IRS has employed a reasonable method of determining income, an “evidentiary link to the specific activity alleged to have generated the income” becomes unnecessary. Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d at 764. Section 6201(d) provides that “[i]n any court proceeding, if a taxpayer asserts a reasonable dispute with respect to any item of income reported on an information return . . . [and] has fully cooperated,” the IRS shall have “the burden of producing reasonable and probative information concerning [the item] in addition to [the] information return.” Section 7491(a)(1) imposes the burden of persuasion on the IRS if the
41
[*41] taxpayer introduces credible evidence, and if the taxpayer proves that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2)(A) and (B)––that the taxpayer has complied with substantiation requirements, maintained all records required, and cooperated with reasonable requests––have been met. Rolfs v. Commissioner, 135 T.C. 471, 483 (2010), aff’d, 668 F.3d 888 (7th Cir. 2012).
Applying these legal principles, we conclude that Pinckney bears the burden of persuasion with respect to the alleged interest income:
First, we hold that the IRS has employed a reasonable method of determining that Pinckney earned unreported interest income. See Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d at 763–64. The IRS introduced records which, as Baumgartner explained in her testimony, indicate that a payor had issued a Form 1099–INT to Pinckney stating that it had paid him $3,326 of interest and that another payor had issued a Form 1099–INT to Pinckney stating that it had paid him $26. These two amounts sum to $3,352. The IRS has thus shown it employed a reasonable method for determining income. See id.
Second, assuming—without deciding—that the IRS is required to make a “minimal evidentiary showing,” id. at 766, that Pinckney is connected with the activity producing the interest income, we hold that the IRS has made this minimal evidentiary showing. See id. As explained above, the IRS introduced its records showing that third parties had issued information returns stating that they had paid interest to Pinckney. These information returns satisfy the minimal evidentiary showing. See Hardy v. Commissioner, 181 F.3d 1002, 1004– 05 (9th Cir. 1999), aff’g T.C. Memo. 1997-97; Rodriguez v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2009-92, 2009 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 93, at *4; Oman v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2010-276, 2010 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 313, at *14–16.
Third, we hold that the IRS is not subject to the additional evidentiary burden imposed by section 6201(d). The amounts of interest in question are items of income reported on information returns within the meaning of section 6201(d). Section 6201(d) would impose an additional evidentiary burden on the IRS had Pinckney asserted a reasonable dispute about the information returns. However, by not asserting any dispute about the information returns, Pinckney failed to assert a “reasonable dispute,” as section 6201(d) requires.
42
[*42] Fourth, we hold that section 7491(a)(1) does not impose the burden of persuasion on the IRS with respect to the interest-income adjustments. Pinckney has introduced no evidence, whether credible or not, as to whether he earned the alleged interest income. Nor has Pinckney shown that he has maintained records or complied with requests as required by the conditions of section 7491(a)(2)(B).15
Having established that Pinckney has the burden of persuasion with respect to the alleged interest income, we now address whether he has satisfied that burden. The burden of persuasion is satisfied if the taxpayer shows that, on the basis of the evidence, the fact in question is more probable than not––that is, by a preponderance of the evidence.
15 Pinckney does not specifically contend that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2)(B) were met with respect to the interest income issue for tax year 2008. However, he obliquely argues (as a general matter, not limited to the interest income issue for tax year 2008) that his records were seized by the government:
At all times relevant, in May 2012, Petitioners [sic] personal and business records were seized by IRS Criminal Investigators from his home and Daitech Tax Services [sic] main location, which consisted of thousands of records and files and has not been returned that [sic] placed him at a disadvantage to sufficiently and equally present his case to the court. Petitioner called the IRS Criminal Investigations Division for the records but later was told by an agent that he will need to file a court subpoena to the United States Federal Court [sic] to attempt to retrieve his business and personal records. Conveniently, the Respondent produced Petitioner records for tax court totally for Respondent’s benefit.
Pinckney does not refer to section 7491, nor does he explain what relief he should be afforded because of the alleged loss of the records. In other instances in which taxpayers have claimed that the IRS has lost documents belonging to them, albeit in cases unrelated to section 7491, we have considered whether to shift the burden of proof from the taxpayer to the IRS. The merits of doing so have depended in part on (1) whether the IRS mishandled the documents, for example, though negligence or violation of agency procedure (as contrasted with unintentional and accidental loss), Am. Police & Fire Found., Inc. v. Commissioner, 81 T.C. 699, 706–09 (1983); (2) whether the taxpayer shared the responsibility for the lost documents, id.; Malinowski v. Commissioner, 71 T.C. 1120, 1125 (1979); and (3) whether the lost documents could have been reconstructed by the taxpayer, Malinowski, 71 T.C. at 1125. Here the trial record contains very little detail regarding how Pinckney’s records were lost, and therefore we are unable to conclude that the IRS mishandled the documents. Nor are we convinced that Pinckney was absolved of responsibility for the documentary loss. Despite the explanation in Pinckney’s brief, the record does not show exactly what steps Pinckney took to retrieve the documents from the IRS. Furthermore, the record does not show what the lost records were, or whether Pinckney could have reconstructed the records. See id. We thus conclude that the alleged loss of the records does not justify shifting the burden of proof to the IRS on the interest income issue for tax year 2008 or any other issue.
43
[*43] Merkel v. Commissioner, 109 T.C. 463, 476 (1997) (citing 2 McCormick on Evidence § 339, at 439 (John W. Strong ed., 4th ed. 1992)), aff’d, 192 F.3d 844 (9th Cir. 1999). Pinckney adduced no evidence to indicate that he did not earn the alleged interest income. We thus find that Pinckney earned $3,352 of unreported interest income.
B. Pinckney Business Consulting earned $463 more in gross receipts than was reported on the relevant Schedule C.
On his 2008 return, Pinckney reported that he operated a sole proprietorship called Pinckney Business Consulting and that the business had gross receipts of $16,057. The Notice of Deficiency determined that its gross receipts were, instead, $16,520, a positive adjustment of $463. We sustain the adjustment.
We conclude that Pinckney bears the burden of persuasion with respect to the gross-receipts adjustment for Pinckney Business Consulting for the following reasons:
First, we hold that the IRS has employed a reasonable method of determining that Pinckney Business Consulting earned $16,520 of business income. Pinckney himself gave the IRS information during the examination that suggested that he earned this amount of business income. In particular, he gave the IRS the following three documents: (1) a one-page summary, generated by computer, showing customer names and related deposits totaling $10,363; (2) a Form 1099–MISC from HSBC Taxpayer Financial Services showing $5,040 was paid to Pinckney; and (3) a Form 1099–MISC showing that $1,117 was paid to Pinckney. It was reasonable for the IRS to use this information for at least two reasons. First, it was against Pinckney’s interest to admit that he received the business income indicated by the documents. Second, the documents have significant indicators of reliability. The one-page deposit summary contains detailed information regarding customer names and amounts and was generated by computer. The Form 1099– MISCs were prepared by third parties. Thus, the IRS has employed a reasonable method for determining income.
Second, assuming––without deciding––that the “minimal evidentiary showing” is required here, we hold that the IRS has established a minimal evidentiary showing connecting Pinckney with the activity producing the business income. See, e.g., Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d at 764–65. As explained above, the IRS introduced three documents it received from Pinckney himself that have
44
[*44] indicia of reliability. These documents satisfy the minimal evidentiary showing. See Hardy v. Commissioner, 181 F.3d at 1004–05; Rodriguez, 2009 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 93, at *4.
Third, we hold that the IRS is not subject to the additional evidentiary burden imposed by section 6201(d). The amounts of business income shown on the two Form 1099–MISCs are “item[s] of income” reported on information returns within the meaning of section 6201(d). Section 6201(d) would have imposed an additional evidentiary burden on the IRS only if Pinckney had asserted a reasonable dispute about the information returns, but Pinckney did not do so.
Fourth, we hold that section 7491(a)(1) does not impose the burden of persuasion on the IRS with respect to the gross-receipts adjustment for Pinckney Business Consulting. Section 7491(a)(1) provides that if, in any court proceeding, the taxpayer introduces credible evidence with respect to any factual issue, the IRS shall have the burden of proof with respect to that issue, provided that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2) are met. Pinckney stipulated the admissibility of the three documents that support the gross-receipts adjustment. But he has not introduced credible evidence contradicting the gross-receipts adjustments as required by section 7491(a)(1).
We now address whether Pinckney has satisfied his burden of persuasion with respect to the adjustment to the gross receipts of Pinckney Business Consulting. Pinckney adduced no evidence to suggest that he did not earn the alleged business income. We therefore sustain the $463 adjustment to the gross receipts of Pinckney Business Consulting.
C. The gross receipts for Daitech are $376,712, rather than $200,500 as reported on Pinckney’s return, or $382,243 as determined in the Notice of Deficiency.
Pinckney reported on his 2008 return that Daitech’s gross receipts were $200,500. The Notice of Deficiency, which was dated February 6, 2019, determined that Daitech’s gross receipts for the year were $382,243. On October 25, 2021, amid this litigation, the IRS acquired bank records regarding the Daitech Account at Community Bank. Baumgartner performed a bank-deposit analysis of the Daitech bank records and, as recorded on spreadsheets admitted at trial, Baumgartner divided the account deposits into taxable and nontaxable deposits, concluding that Daitech had taxable deposits of $380,200 for
45
[*45] tax year 2008. This amount was $2,043 lower than the $382,243 gross-receipts amount determined in the Notice of Deficiency. At trial, the IRS offered no method for calculating Daitech’s gross receipts other than Baumgartner’s bank-deposit analysis.
When the IRS’s determination of a deficiency rests upon a bankdeposit analysis, the taxpayer has the burden of proving that a particular deposit came from a nontaxable source. Clayton v. Commissioner, 102 T.C. 632, 645 (1994); accord Marcello v. Commissioner, 380 F.2d 494, 496–97 (5th Cir. 1967) (citing, inter alia, Price v. United States, 335 F.2d 671, 678 (5th Cir. 1964)), aff’g in part and rev’g in part on other grounds T.C. Memo. 1964-302. This is consistent with the general rule that the taxpayer has the burden of proving that the IRS’s determination of a deficiency is incorrect. Rule 142(a). However, the IRS has the burden of proof as to a new matter not found in the notice of deficiency. Id. A new matter is a matter that “reasonably would also alter the evidence presented.” Greenberg v. Commissioner, 10 F.4th 1136, 1172 (11th Cir. 2021) (quoting Hurst v. Commissioner, 124 T.C. 16, 30 (2005)), aff’g T.C. Memo. 2018-74; Shea v. Commissioner, 112 T.C. 183, 197 (1999) (“[A] new matter is raised when the basis or theory on which the Commissioner relies was not stated or described in the notice of deficiency and the new theory or basis requires the presentation of different evidence.”).
Baumgartner’s bank-deposit analysis is properly considered a new matter. No mention of a bank-deposit analysis is found in the Notice of Deficiency. Like the Notice of Deficiency, the 2018 30-day letter concluded that Daitech’s gross receipts were $382,243. The 2018 30-day letter stated how the IRS had computed Daitech’s gross receipts to be $382,243. It stated that Daitech’s gross receipts were the sum of two amounts: $201,880 “[p]er information provided by taxpayer” and $180,362.77 “[p]er [the] indictment.” The origin of the first amount, $201,880, is not revealed by the record. The number is very close to the amount reported by Pinckney himself on his tax return. There is no indication that this reported amount came from an analysis of Daitech’s bank statements. The second amount, $180,362.77, is the amount that the superseding grand jury indictment alleged was Pinckney’s taxable income for 2008. There is no indication that this amount came from an analysis of Daitech’s bank statements, either.16
16 In this paragraph, we have discussed material from the 2018 30-day letter and the superseding grand jury indictment. Respondent did not object to the
46
[*46] We conclude that the bank-deposit analysis was not described in the Notice of Deficiency. Furthermore, the analysis requires the presentation of different evidence from the Notice of Deficiency because it requires evidence about deposits into the Daitech Account. Therefore, we hold that the IRS bears the burden of persuasion as to whether a particular deposit into the Daitech Account is includable in Pinckney’s gross income.
So far, we have not discussed whether the IRS must establish a “minimal evidentiary showing” connecting Pinckney with the activity generating the alleged income constituting the bank deposits that Baumgartner determined to be taxable. Assuming––without deciding–– that the IRS is required to establish a “minimal evidentiary showing” connecting Pinckney with the activity generating the alleged income, see, e.g., Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d at 764, we hold that this showing was met. The activity generating the alleged income is the Daitech business, and the record shows that Pinckney owned and operated Daitech. However, as discussed before, the IRS has the burden of persuading us that a particular bank deposit is includable in gross income. We now consider whether the IRS has satisfied this burden of persuasion.
