[*55] In summary, Baumgartner made the following conclusions:
Daitech Account––2008
Includable
in gross
Not includable
income
in gross
income TaxFin Services Fee Pymnt $351,323 Tax Refund Pro Raldept 8,237 Tax Prep 4,117 Cash 12,910 Other 3,613 Non-Taxable 192,680 Total $380,200 $192,680
Each deposit in Baumgartner’s first category of deposits, “TaxFin Services Fee Pymnt,” was described as “TaxFin Services Fee Pymnt” on the bank statements. Baumgartner concluded that these deposits were income from Daitech’s operations as a tax-return preparer. We find that a preponderance of the evidence supports Baumgartner’s classification of the deposits as taxable. However, we find that the total that Baumgartner calculated for the deposits in this category, $351,323, is $130 too low from the correct amount of $351,453. We believe that this amount arose from a simple arithmetic error.
The second category of deposits, “Tax Refund Pro Raldept,” consists of all deposits described as “Tax Refund Pro Raldept” on the bank statements. Baumgartner concluded that these deposits, too, were income from Daitech’s operations as a tax-return preparer. We find that a preponderance of the evidence supports Baumgartner’s classification of the deposits as taxable.
The third category of deposits, “Tax Prep,” consists of deposits that had the following characteristics: (1) the bank statement described the deposit as a check deposit, rather than a direct deposit; (2) the Daitech Account records contained a copy of a check that corresponded to the deposit and (a) the check was written by a Daitech customer, and (b) the check had a memo line that contained a description similar to “Tax Prep” or “taxes.”17 Baumgartner concluded that these deposits, too,
17 One check in this category did not have a memo line with a description similar to “taxes” or “Tax Prep.” However, the check was written by a Daitech customer and was in an amount that was the same as the fees paid by other Daitech customers for tax-return preparation.
56
[*56] were income. We find that a preponderance of the evidence supports Baumgartner’s classification of the deposits as taxable.
The fourth category of deposits, “Cash,” consists of all deposits for which the bank records included a corresponding First Community Bank printout titled “Cash In Ticket” prepared by a bank teller. A “Cash In Ticket” was prepared for all deposits made in cash.18 Baumgartner testified that the cash deposits were payments for services, due to the nature of the Daitech business, and thus, that these deposits were income. We find that a preponderance of the evidence supports Baumgartner’s classification of these deposits as taxable. However, we believe that the total that Baumgartner calculated for the deposits in this category, $12,910, is $5 too high. We believe that this error relates to one deposit. The bank statement for this deposit stated that the amount was $715. While consistent with Baumgartner’s total of $100 in cash and $615 in checks, this is inconsistent with the facsimiles of the checks (showing that the amounts of checks deposited totaled $610) plus the Cash In Deposit slip (showing the cash deposited was $100), as well as what appears to be a correction slip stating the corrected amount of the deposit as $100 in cash and $610 in checks. This $5 error also affected the $380,200 total on Baumgartner’s spreadsheet (the sum of the first five categories), which should have been, including the arithmetic error, $380,195 (and $380,325 excluding it).
For the fifth category, “Other,” which Baumgartner classified as taxable, Baumgartner explained in her testimony that these deposits were those whose source she could not clearly discern. We conclude there is not enough evidence in the record to satisfy the IRS’s burden of persuasion that these deposits are taxable. We conclude that these deposits are not includable in gross income.
For deposits in the sixth category, “Non-Taxable,” Baumgartner concluded that these deposits were not includable in Pinckney’s gross income. The IRS does not contest this conclusion. Therefore, we need not resolve whether the deposits are includable in Pinckney’s gross income. We assume that they are not.
18 For each cash deposit, the bank records contain not only a Cash In Ticket, but also a deposit slip. The deposit slip showed the total amount of cash deposited for each instance in which cash was deposited and also showed the total amount of all checks (if any) that were deposited at the same time the cash was deposited.
57
[*57] Our conclusions, therefore, are as follows:
Daitech Account––2008
Includable
in gross
Not includable
income
in gross
income TaxFin Services Fee Pymnt $351,453 Tax Refund Pro Raldept 8,237 Tax Prep 4,117 Cash 12,905 Other $3,613 Non-Taxable 192,680 Total $376,712 $196,293
In conclusion, in our view of the record, $376,712 of the deposits into the Daitech Account are includable in gross income for 2008.
In forming our views, we are unpersuaded by the following arguments given by Pinckney as to why this Court should wholly reject Baumgartner’s use of the bank-deposit method:
1. Pinckney argues that Baumgartner was “coached” by counsel for the IRS. The impetus for this criticism appears to be that Baumgartner explained that IRS counsel had asked her to perform the bank-deposits analysis on the Daitech Account records. Neither this fact, nor the record as a whole, suggests that IRS counsel improperly coached Baumgartner’s testimony.
2. Pinckney argues that Baumgartner did not understand the difference between the bank-deposit method and what Pinckney called the “net worth of proof method.” At trial, Pinckney had asked Baumgartner questions about the “net worth of proof method.” Baumgartner did not initially understand what Pinckney was referring to. But this was because the method Pinckney was asking about is usually referred to as the “net worth method.” E.g., Hurley v. Commissioner, 22 T.C. 1256, 1260 (1954) (stating that the IRS used the “net worth method”), aff’d, 233 F.2d 177 (6th Cir. 1956).
3. Pinckney contends that Baumgartner failed to consider the payments made from the Daitech Account. Pinckney
58
[*58]
explained this argument as follows: “She [Baumgartner] did not complete a true Bank Deposit Method of proof because she only focused on deposits and not expenditures. An accurate and true Bank Deposit Analysis must consider the two.” Pinckney’s argument has no merit. In employing the bankdeposit method, “the Government must take into account any . . . deductible expense of which it has knowledge,” but it is not required to follow leads suggesting that the taxpayer has a deductible expense. DiLeo v. Commissioner, 96 T.C. 858, 872 (1991) (first quoting Price, 335 F.2d at 677; and then citing Marcello v. Commissioner, 380 F.2d at 497), aff’d, 959 F.2d 16 (2d Cir. 1992). Furthermore, the taxpayer bears the burden of proving that a particular expense is deductible. Id. Even if a bank-deposit analysis does not confirm a deduction to which the taxpayer is entitled, this does not invalidate the method in its entirety. Marcello v. Commissioner, 380 F.2d at 497 (holding that even if “the Commissioner’s determination was not completely correct[, this] does not invalidate the method employed”). Applying these principles, we reject Pinckney’s argument. First, the record does not demonstrate that Baumgartner had “knowledge” that any payments from the Daitech Account were deductible. DiLeo, 96 T.C. at 872. Although we hold that $72,906 of the payments from the Daitech Account in 2008 are deductible, see infra Part I.F, this means only that we believe, based on the trial record, that it is more probable than not that the payments are the business expenses of Daitech. Merkel, 109 T.C. at 476 (“The usual measure of persuasion required to prove a fact in this Court is ‘preponderance of the evidence,’ which means that the proponent must prove that the fact is more probable than not.” (citations omitted) (quoting, inter alia, Schaffer v. Commissioner, 779 F.2d 849, 858 (2d Cir. 1985), aff’g in part Mandina v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 1982-34)). This is different from a finding that Baumgartner had “knowledge” that the payments were deductible. Second, Baumgartner was not required to follow any “leads” suggesting that the payments were deductible. See DiLeo, 96 T.C. at 872. And third, even if Baumgartner erred in failing to determine that some of the payments from the Daitech Account were deductible, this type of error does not justify disregarding
59
[*59]
deposits into the bank account. Marcello v. Commissioner, 380 F.2d at 497.19
Thus, as none of Pinckney’s arguments withstand scrutiny, and the IRS has proven by a preponderance of the evidence that the gross receipts of Daitech were $376,712, the determination in the Notice of Deficiency—that Daitech’s gross receipts were $382,243—is sustained to the extent of $376,712.
D. The business-expense deductions for Pinckney Business Consulting are $3,313, not $8,089 as reported on the Schedule C.
Included along with Pinckney’s Form 1040 for his 2008 return, Pinckney attached a Schedule C for Pinckney Business Consulting. On this Schedule C, he claimed $8,089 of business-expense deductions comprising the following categories: $3,313 of car-and-truck-expense deductions; $2,415 of office-expense deductions; $1,061 of meals-andentertainment-expense deductions; and $1,300 of utilities-expense deductions. The Notice of Deficiency disallowed the last three categories of deductions, for a total disallowance of $4,776. Thus, the Notice of Deficiency allowed $3,313 of deductions for the business expenses of Pinckney Business Consulting.
Personal expenses are nondeductible under section 262(a). Section 162(a) allows a taxpayer to deduct all ordinary and necessary expenses incurred in carrying on a trade or business. The term “ordinary” principally functions to distinguish expenses that are currently deductible from those that are capital expenses. Commissioner v. Tellier, 383 U.S. 687, 689–90 (1966). An expense is “necessary” if it is “appropriate and helpful” in the taxpayer’s business, but it need not be
19 An argument Pinckney does not make is that the bank-deposit method included amounts that had also been determined by the Notice of Deficiency to be includable in Pinckney’s income through adjustments in the Notice of Deficiency other than the adjustments to Daitech’s gross receipts. For 2008, the Notice of Deficiency determined that Pinckney had $3,326 of income from One West Bank FSB and $26 from Carolina Collegiate Federal Credit Union. None of the deposits to the Daitech Account for 2008 appear to correspond to these amounts. As explained above, Pinckney does not contend that the bank-deposits method results in double counting of income, which is consistent with Pinckney’s testimony that the Daitech Account received the business income of Daitech (as opposed to income from other sources). Similarly, Pinckney does not make a double counting argument for 2009, and none of the increases to income in the Notice of Deficiency (other than to the Daitech Schedule C) appear to correspond to any deposits into the Daitech Account for that year.
60
[*60] essential. Id. at 689 (quoting Welch v. Helvering, 290 U.S. 111, 113 (1933)). A taxpayer is required to maintain sufficient records to establish the amounts of allowable deductions and to enable the IRS to determine his or her correct tax liability. § 6001; Treas. Reg. § 1.6001-1(a). Under the Cohan rule, if a taxpayer establishes that he or she incurred a deductible expense but is unable to substantiate the precise amount, a court should estimate the amount of the allowable deduction, bearing heavily against the taxpayer whose inexactitude is of his or her own making. Cohan v. Commissioner, 39 F.2d 540, 543–44 (2d Cir. 1930). For a court to estimate the amount of the allowable deduction under the Cohan rule, there must be sufficient evidence in the record to provide a basis for such an estimate. Vanicek v. Commissioner, 85 T.C. 731, 742– 43 (1985). Section 274(d) supersedes the Cohan rule with respect to some deductions, imposing strict substantiation requirements for deductions related to travel, meals, and lodging while away from home, entertainment, gifts, and “listed property” (a term defined by section 280F(d)(4) to include passenger automobiles). Sanford v. Commissioner, 50 T.C. 823, 827–28 (1968), aff’d per curiam, 412 F.2d 201 (2d Cir. 1969).
Here, Pinckney has the burden of persuasion as to his entitlement to the deductions for a number of reasons: First, the determination in the Notice of Deficiency disallowing the deductions is presumed correct, Welch v. Helvering, 290 U.S. at 115; Cozzi, 88 T.C. at 443 & n.8; second, Pinckney has not introduced credible evidence that would satisfy the requirement of section 7491(a)(1); and third, Pinckney has not shown that the conditions imposed by section 7491(a)(2)(B) have been met. We now consider whether Pinckney has satisfied the burden of persuasion.
The record contains no testimony addressing whatever payments for which the contested deductions have been claimed. As for documentary evidence, the only documentary evidence of payments made by Pinckney is the bank records for the Daitech Account. But according to Pinckney, payments from this bank account related to the business expenses of Daitech, rather than Pinckney Business Consulting. Therefore, the preponderance of the evidence does not support Pinckney’s entitlement to any deductions, nor does it support this Court’s engaging in a Cohan rule estimation of any kind as to the proper allowable amount of deductions.
61
[*61]
E. Daitech’s cost of goods sold is zero, not $23,231 as reported on Daitech’s Schedule C.
Pinckney reported on the Schedule C for Daitech that Daitech’s cost of goods sold was $23,231. The Notice of Deficiency determined that the amount was zero. We sustain this determination.
Taxable income is equal to the gross income minus deductions, § 63(a), one type of which is the deduction under section 162(a) for business expenses. When a taxpayer earns gross income from selling goods, the gross income from those sales is equal to the receipts from the sales (total sales) minus the cost of goods sold. § 61(a)(2); Treas. Reg. § 1.61-3(a). Cost of goods sold is calculated as the sum of the cost of beginning inventory and the cost of purchases (and other acquisition or production costs) during the tax year less the cost of ending inventory. Huffman v. Commissioner, 126 T.C. 322, 324 (2006), aff’d, 518 F.3d 357 (6th Cir. 2008); see also Treas. Reg. §§ 1.162-1(a), 1.446-1(a)(4)(i), 1.471-1.
Pinckney has the burden of persuasion as to the amount of costof-goods-sold. This is because (1) the determination in the Notice of Deficiency that the amount is zero is presumed correct, Cozzi, 88 T.C. at 443 & n.8, 444; (2) Pinckney has not introduced credible evidence that would satisfy the requirement of section 7491(a)(1); and (3) Pinckney has not shown that the conditions imposed by section 7491(a)(2)(B) have been met.
The record contains no evidence that Daitech sold any goods. The record instead shows that Daitech was in the business of filing tax returns, which is a service. Therefore, Pinckney has not satisfied his burden of persuasion as to cost of goods sold.
F. The Notice of Deficiency did not err in determining that the business-expense deductions for Daitech are $132,444, not $141,883 as reported on the Schedule C.
Pinckney’s return for 2008 included a Schedule C for Daitech. On this Schedule C, he claimed $141,883 of business-expense deductions divided into various categories corresponding to the items on the Schedule C. The Notice of Deficiency allowed $132,444 of businessexpense deductions for Daitech, dividing these allowed deductions into various categories corresponding to the line items on the Schedule C.
62
[*62] Section 162(a) allows a taxpayer to deduct all ordinary and necessary expenses incurred in carrying on a trade or business. An expense is “necessary” if it is “appropriate and helpful” in the taxpayer’s business, but it need not be essential. Commissioner v. Tellier, 383 U.S. at 689 (quoting Welch v. Helvering, 290 U.S. at 113). A taxpayer is required to maintain sufficient records to establish the amounts of allowable deductions and to enable the IRS to determine his or her correct tax liability. § 6001; Treas. Reg. § 1.6001-1(a). Under the Cohan rule, if a taxpayer establishes that he or she paid a deductible expense but is unable to substantiate the precise amount, a court should estimate the amount of the allowable deduction, bearing heavily against the taxpayer whose inexactitude is of his or her own making. Cohan v. Commissioner, 39 F.2d at 543–44. For a court to estimate the amount of the allowable deduction under the Cohan rule, there must be sufficient evidence in the record to provide a basis for such an estimate. Vanicek, 85 T.C. at 742–43. Section 274(d) supersedes the Cohan rule with respect to some deductions, imposing strict substantiation requirements for deductions related to travel, meals, and lodging while away from home, entertainment, gifts, and “listed property” (a term defined in section 280F(d)(4) to include passenger automobiles). Sanford, 50 T.C. at 827–28.
Section 280A(a) provides that no deductions are allowed with respect to a dwelling unit used as the taxpayer’s residence. There are exceptions to this rule, such as for an expense allocable to a portion of a dwelling unit used exclusively or on a regular basis as a place of business used by clients in meeting or dealing with the taxpayer in the normal course of business. § 280A(c)(1)(B).
Pinckney has the burden of persuasion as to the deductions. This is because (1) the determination in the Notice of Deficiency to disallow the deductions is presumed correct, Cozzi, 88 T.C. at 443 & n.8, 444, (2) Pinckney has not introduced credible evidence that would satisfy the requirement of section 7491(a)(1), and (3) Pinckney has not shown that the conditions imposed by section 7491(a)(2)(B) and (C) have been met.
To show an error in the determination in the Notice of Deficiency, Pinckney must prove that the total allowable business-expense deductions regarding Daitech exceed the $132,444 total businessexpense deductions regarding Daitech allowed by the Notice of Deficiency. Although the Notice of Deficiency broke down this $132,444 of deductions allowed into 13 different categories (e.g., $3,486 was allowed for “Advertising” expenses), the record does not disclose which
63
[*63] particular payments were included by the IRS in which of the 13 categories. For example, the Notice of Deficiency allowed $3,486 for “Advertising” expense, but we do not know which payments were included in this $3,486 total. Thus, if Pinckney were to prove that Daitech paid $20,000 in deductible expenses that were advertising expenses, this alone would not be enough to show error in the determination in the Notice of Deficiency. All $20,000 of the expenses could have already been allowed by the Notice of Deficiency as deductions under one of the other 12 categories. See Wathen v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2021-100, at *24 & n.8.
To evaluate whether Pinckney has proven that Daitech had more than $132,444 in business-expense deductions for 2008, we consult the sole source of documentary evidence potentially relating to these deductions, the bank records regarding the Daitech Account. The chart below sets forth all the information from the bank records regarding payments from the Daitech Account.

In addition to the bank records of the Daitech Account, Pinckney’s testimony is a relevant source of information, and so we take into account his testimony in our determination of the amounts of the deductions.
For most of the payments from the Daitech Account, neither the bank records nor Pinckney’s testimony shows the connection between the payment and Daitech. In this abundant class of payments, we include the following types of payments:
Payments for which we know from the bank records what
product and service was purchased, but for which there is no indication in the trial record how the product and service related to Daitech. So, for example, the bank records show that on January 22, 2008, Pinckney paid $148 to State Farm Insurance. This payment likely was for insurance coverage. But we do not have an indication in the trial record that the insurance coverage related to Daitech.
Payments for which Pinckney’s testimony was merely
conclusory as to business purpose. For example, IRS counsel asked Pinckney whether the $1,018 check written to Plexus on April 2, 2008, was a business expense, Pinckney replied, “That’s totally business.”
Payments that Pinckney testified were business expenses
merely because they were paid out of the Daitech Account. For example, when IRS counsel asked Pinckney whether the $149.64 payment to Rugged Wearhouse on April 17, 2008, was a business expense, he responded: “If it’s on the business statement, it’s purchased for business.”
93
[*93]
Payments about which Pinckney gave no testimony at all.
Pinckney testified about the payments from the Daitech Account only when asked about them by IRS counsel. For payments about which IRS counsel asked no question, there is no testimony in the record. Furthermore, Pinckney refused to answer questions by IRS counsel about some payments on Fifth Amendment grounds. For these payments, too, there is no testimony in the trial record.20
As to this class of payments, we find that none were the ordinary and necessary expenses of carrying on the business of Daitech. The mere fact that the expenses were paid out of the Daitech Account is insufficient to carry Pinckney’s burden of proving that they are business expenses. It appears that it was Pinckney alone who decided whether to pay the expenses out of the Daitech Account. We discerned little indication that Pinckney distinguished between personal expenses and business expenses when doing so. That the Daitech Account was not limited to business expenses is suggested by the sheer number of payments for clothing, entertainment, and food.
We now proceed to the second class of payments. These are payments for which there is some arguable indication, either from documents or testimony, that there is a connection to Daitech. An exhaustive list of such payments follows:
A January 17, 2008,21 check for $3,000 was written to “First
National Bank of Omaha.” Pinckney testified that this payment was for “a credit card for the business.” We do not know, however, what was purchased with this credit card. None of the credit card statements for this credit card were made a part of the record. We have little reliable indication that the purchases with the credit card were related to Daitech’s business. The purchases with the credit card could be personal expenses, and given that Pinckney has the burden of proof, we conclude they were indeed personal purchases.
20 As explained infra Part VII.C, our findings of fact are made without drawing adverse inferences from Pinckney’s refusals to testify.
21 These check dates refer to the date on which the check was written, rather than the date the check was debited. The January 17, 2008, check for $3,000, for example, was written on January 17, 2008, and debited on January 25, 2008.
94
[*94]
A January 17, 2008, check for $75.36 was written to “Blue
Cross Blue Shield.” Pinckney testified that this was “insurance payments for . . . my healthcare as a business owner” and that furthermore the payment involved the “business providing healthcare to the employee which was me.” Pinckney’s testimony that the payment related to Daitech’s business relationship with himself as either an owner or an employee does not show that the payment was deductible. Daitech was a sole proprietorship of Pinckney’s. Pinckney cannot be his own employee. Thus, a payment by Daitech to benefit its sole proprietor, Pinckney, is nondeductible.
A February 11, 2008, check for $2,000 was written to
“Discover.” Pinckney testified that “it’s probably for the business” but that he couldn’t “recollect for sure.” As with the January 17, 2008, First National Bank of Omaha check, Pinckney has not overcome the burden of proof.
A February 25, 2008, check for $67.56 was written to “Blue
Cross Blue Shield.” Pinckney testified that this was “[p]robably [for the] insurance plan for the employees.” But there is no corroborating evidence that the insurance plan in question covered Daitech employees as opposed to Pinckney himself. We do not believe Pinckney’s tentative and vague testimony on this payment.
A March 4, 2008, check for $289.75 was written to “TrueGreen
Lawn Services.” Pinckney testified that the check “look[ed]” like it could be with my home . . . where I did conduct a lot of business.” Pinckney’s testimony that the payment related to his house does not prove his entitlement to a business-expense deduction. Section 280A(a) provides that “in the case of a taxpayer who is an individual . . . , no deduction . . . [is] allowed with respect to the use of a dwelling unit which is used by the taxpayer during the taxable year as a residence.” Although there are exceptions, for example, for an expense allocable to a portion of the dwelling unit that is exclusively used on a regular basis as a place of business, e.g., § 280A(c)(1)(B), Pinckney has not proven that a portion of his house was used exclusively on a regular basis as a place of business for Daitech’s customers to meet or deal with him. See id. Nor has Pinckney proven that any other exception to section 280A(a)
95
[*95]
is applicable. Therefore, an expense related to his house is not deductible by operation of section 280A(a).
An April 4, 2008, payment for $58.81 was made to “Michaels.”
Pinckney testified: “We did decoration [sic]. . . . We had three offices to put together.” This testimony implies that the payment to Michaels was an office expense. But without further specific or corroborating evidence, we are unconvinced by Pinckney’s vague and nonspecific testimony.
An April 8, 2008, check was written for $810 to “Ascot HOA.”
IRS counsel asked whether the check was for home association dues that were personal expenses. Pinckney responded: “I was conducting a lot of business at home.” But Pinckney has not shown that there is an applicable exception to section 280A(a) for this expense.
An April 17, 2008, check was written for $2,066 to “State
Farm” with a subject line of “home insurance.” Pinckney testified: “That’s probably for business use of home.” But again, Pinckney has not shown that there is an applicable exception to section 280A(a) for this expense.
An April 17, 2008, check was written for $372 to “State Farm
Insurance” for “business car insurance.” Without any specifics about what car was used for the Daitech business, and without information about Pinckney’s other vehicles, we are unable to conclude on a preponderance of the evidence that the expense was for a business vehicle.
An April 18, 2008, check was written for $640 to “Native
Plants.” When IRS counsel asked whether this was a “personal expense for planting at your house,” Pinckney testified: “No. That’s business. Like I said, I conducted business at home.” But Pinckney has not shown that there is an applicable exception to section 280A(a) for this expense.
A May 5, 2008, payment for $74.84 was made to “Lowe’s.” As
to this expense, Pinckney testified that Daitech’s offices were “old buildings and I had to do a lot of work to them.” While it is plausible that Pinckney might have spent some money maintaining the leased premises used by Daitech, there is no specific evidence of what was purchased with the $74.84
96
[*96]
payment or which, if any, of Daitech’s offices the purchase was related to. Under these circumstances, Pinckney has not satisfied his burden of proving that the $74.84 payment was a business expense of Daitech.
A May 18, 2008, check for $500 was written to “Indymac.” The
memo line of the check stated that the payment related to a “real estate loan.” Pinckney testified that the payment “probably” stemmed from a loan related to his house. On his Schedule A for 2008, Pinckney claimed a $69,981 deduction for home mortgage interest, $39,667 of which was allowed by the IRS. Section 163(a) allows a deduction for “all interest paid or accrued within the taxable year on indebtedness.” Although section 163(h)(1) provides that noncorporate taxpayers cannot deduct any “personal interest,” section 163(h)(2)(D) provides that “personal interest” does not include “any qualified residence interest.” “Qualified residence interest” includes “acquisition indebtedness with respect to any qualified residence of the taxpayer.” § 163(h)(3)(A). A “qualified residence” includes “the principal residence . . . of the taxpayer.” § 163(h)(5)(A)(i)(I). A deduction permitted as qualified residence interest under section 163(h)(2)(D) is an itemized deduction reportable on Schedule A. As a section 163(h)(2)(D) deduction, the deduction is not subject to the section 280A(a) rule, which disallows deductions with respect to the use of a dwelling unit that is used by the taxpayer as a residence. See §§ 280A(b) (exempting from disallowance a deduction, such as a home-mortgage-interest deduction, that is allowable to a taxpayer without regard to the taxpayer’s business), 63(d); Hamacher v. Commissioner, 94 T.C. 348, 355 n.4 (1990). Interest can also be deducted as a business expense, reportable on Schedule C. See §§ 162(a), 62(a)(1), (4); Standing v. Commissioner, 28 T.C. 789, 795 (1957), aff’d, 259 F.2d 450 (4th Cir. 1958); McNutt-Boyce Co. v. Commissioner, 38 T.C. 462, 464 (1962), aff’d per curiam, 324 F.2d 957 (5th Cir. 1963). But a claimed business-expense deduction related to the use of a dwelling unit that is used by the taxpayer as a residence must still satisfy an exception to section 280A(a). Pinckney has not shown that the section 280A(c)(1)(B) exception, or any other exception to section 280A(a), is applicable to the expenses of his house as a business-expense deduction. Although Pinckney’s $500 payment to IndyMac might be deductible as an itemized
97
[*97]
deduction under section 163(h)(2)(D) (to the extent it is a payment of interest), the record does not show that the deduction has not already been allowed as part of the $39,667 amount allowed in the Notice of Deficiency as an itemized home mortgage interest deduction. See Wathen, T.C. Memo. 2021-100, at *24 & n.8.
On May 19, 2008, payment for $53.49 was made to “Best Buy.”
Questioned about this payment, Pinckney testified that the purchase was for a business purpose because it was paid from the Daitech Account. He further testified: “Things have to be purchased. I had TVs.” Without more specific evidence about what the alleged televisions were used for, and where they were located, we are not convinced that the payment to Best Buy was a business expense of Daitech.
A May 19, 2008, check for $41,469.12 was written to
“SunTrust.” The memo line to the check stated: “Car pay off [sic], $41,000.” Pinckney testified that he had personally owned a car, that Daitech bought the car from him, that as part of the purchase transaction Daitech paid off the loan on the car, and that the car was used then for business purposes. However, we need not discuss all the requirements for deducting expenses of the car as the business expenses of Daitech because Pinckney failed to substantiate the use to which the car was put in 2008. Section 274(d) provides that a taxpayer must substantiate the amount of a car expense and the time, place, and business purpose of the use of the car. See §§ 274(d), 280F(d)(4)(A)(i) and (ii), (5)(A). However, a taxpayer may opt to use the standard mileage rate, as established by the IRS for a given tax year, in lieu of substantiating the amounts of the actual expenses. See Treas. Reg. § 1.274-5(j)(2). A taxpayer who opts to use the standard mileage rate is relieved of the obligation to substantiate the amount of the expense but is not relieved of the obligation to substantiate the amount of the business use of the car (i.e., the amount of business mileage), the time of the use of the car, and the business purpose of the use. See id. Pinckney did not introduce substantiation of the amount of the business use of the car, the time of the use of the car, and the business purpose for which the car was used. Thus, section 274(d) precludes a deduction for the use of the car.
98
[*98]
On May 28, 2008, a payment of $71.78 was made to “Aldi.”
Pinckney testified that Aldi was a grocery store, that “we” shop at Aldi, and that “I’m always feeding my people, feeding the customer.” Pinckney’s testimony that this $71.78 payment was for food for Daitech customers and employees is not credible. One of Pinckney’s employees testified that he did not serve food to his customers. We believe this testimony over Pinckney’s testimony that he provided food to his customers. Furthermore, even if Pinckney provided food to his customers or employees, we are not persuaded that this payment was for such food (as opposed to food for Pinckney’s personal consumption or for some nonfood item).
A May 29, 2008, check was written for $280 to “Antoinette
Pinckney.” The memo line for the check stated that the payment was a “[r]eimbursement.” Pinckney testified that he could not recall what she was being reimbursed for. However, he stated that the payment was a “direct business expense” because she worked for Daitech. Although Antoinette Pinckney was an employee of Daitech,22 this memo line is insufficient to convince us that the payment was a business expense of Daitech. The record does not contain any information about what expense was being reimbursed for.
A June 5, 2008, check for $834 was written to the “SunTrust
Bank” regarding a “business loan.” If this loan was a loan to Daitech, we do not know whether the $834 was a payment of principal or interest. A repayment of principal by a borrower is nondeductible.
A July 3, 2008, check for $833 was written to the “SunTrust
Bank.” Pinckney testified: “That’s probably with SunTrust with the car, or one of the cars.” Pinckney’s testimony that the payment related to a Daitech car is insufficient. As explained above with respect to the May 19, 2008, check to SunTrust, Pinckney failed to supply substantiation regarding the use to which any car was put in 2008.
A July 15, 2008, check for $70,000 was written to Pinckney
himself. Pinckney testified that the payment was “[p]robably for . . . investment for Daitech.” He further testified that it was
22 Pinckney would later testify that Antoinette Pinckney was his sister-in-law.
99
[*99]
probably for “some type of CD annuity of some sort” but that he “c[ould]n’t recollect.” This vague testimony is insufficient to convince us that the payment was related to an investment for Daitech (as opposed to a transfer to Pinckney for his personal use). Furthermore, even if we believed that the $70,000 was an investment for Daitech, the appropriate tax treatment of the payment would be to capitalize the payment rather than expense it. See § 263(a).
A July 25, 2008, check for $400 was written to “Antoinette
Pinckney.” Pinckney testified that “this was a business loan from Daitech to Antoinette Pinckney.” He further testified that Antoinette Pinckney was his sister-in-law. This testimony is insufficient to convince us that the $400 payment was a loan extended by Daitech (as opposed to a loan from Pinckney or an expenditure for some nonloan purpose). But even if the $400 was a loan from Daitech, the appropriate tax treatment would not be as a deductible business expense. See MacMillan v. Commissioner, 14 B.T.A. 1367, 1370–71 (1929).
An August 18, 2008, check for $1,000 was written to
“Antoinette Pinckney.” Pinckney testified that the payment was a loan from Daitech. This testimony is insufficient to convince us that the $1,000 payment was a loan extended by Daitech (as opposed to a loan from Pinckney or an expenditure for some nonloan purpose). But even if the $1,000 was a loan from Daitech, the appropriate tax treatment would not be as a deductible business expense. See id.
On September 30, 2008, a $10,000 check was written to
“E-Trade Bank.” Pinckney testified that the payment was an “[i]nvestment for the company.” This vague testimony is insufficient to convince us that the payment was related to an investment for Daitech (as opposed to a payment for an investment for Pinckney personally or a payment for a noninvestment other purpose). Furthermore, even if we believed that the $10,000 payment was an investment for Daitech, the appropriate tax treatment of the payment would be to capitalize the payment rather than expense it. See § 263(a).
On October 23, 2008, a $147.83 check was written to “State
Farm.” Pinckney testified that the payment was “probably” for
100
[*100]
car insurance for “the business cars.” As explained above, Pinckney failed to supply contemporaneous documentation regarding the use to which any car was put in 2008. Nor are we persuaded that the payment was for car insurance (as opposed to some other type of insurance).
There are several payments from the Daitech Account that are
designated for payroll in one of four ways. First, the bank statement could describe the payment with the word “Payroll.” Second, the bank statement could describe the payment with the word “Wages.” Third, the bank statement could describe the payment with the words “Payroll Service Fee.” Fourth, a check via which the payment was made could have the notation that it was for “Payroll.” In total, these payrollassociated payments are $56,618. This total does not include a $10,000 payment to Eric Pinckney of “Bonus wages” on August 14, 2008, because a taxpayer cannot deduct wages he paid to himself. We conclude that $56,618 of the 2008 payments from the Daitech Account is deductible as business expenses of Daitech.
There are several payments from the Daitech Account to D&H
Investments and to Tobin Company. These payments were made by check. Many of the checks for 2008 and 2009 contain a memo line noting that the payments are for “office” rent, noting that the payments were for “office” utilities, or noting a suite number. We surmise that all of the payments to D&H Investments and Tobin Company are payments of rent or utilities on Daitech offices. We conclude that these payments are deductible. For 2008 the total payments to D&H Investments were $8,400. The total payments to Tobin Company were $7,888. We hold that the sum of these amounts, $16,288 (i.e., $8,400 + $7,888), is deductible as a business expense of Daitech.
In summary, we have divided the payments from the Daitech Account during 2008 into two classes. The second class of payments, which are enumerated payment by payment in the discussion above, consists of those payments for which the evidence arguably points to a connection to the operation of Daitech. As to this second class of payments, we conclude that $72,906 are deductible. The first class of payments, which are definitionally all payments from the Daitech Account except for the second class of payments, is not deductible.
101
[*101] Pinckney has proven only $72,906 of deductible business expenses of Daitech. Therefore, he has failed to prove that the deductible amount is more than the $132,444 amount determined in the Notice of Deficiency. We therefore sustain the Notice of Deficiency as to this determination.
G. As determined by the Notice of Deficiency, the amount of Pinckney’s home-mortgage-interest deduction for 2008 is $39,667.
On his 2008 return, Pinckney claimed a $69,981 deduction for home-mortgage interest. The Notice of Deficiency allowed only $39,667 of this deduction.
Pinckney has the burden of persuasion as to the disallowed portion of this deduction. This is because (1) the determination in the Notice of Deficiency to partially disallow the deduction is presumed correct, Cozzi, 88 T.C. at 443 & n.8, 444; (2) Pinckney has not introduced credible evidence that his home mortgage expense exceeded $39,667 and thus he has not satisfied the requirement of section 7491(a)(1); and (3) Pinckney has not shown that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2)(B) have been met.
A taxpayer is generally allowed to deduct interest paid on debt secured by the taxpayer’s principal residence. See § 163(a), (h)(2)(D), (h)(3), (h)(4)(A)(i).
No evidence shows that Pinckney paid more than $39,667 of interest on the mortgage on his house in Irmo, South Carolina.23 Therefore he has not satisfied his burden of persuasion as to the disallowed portion of the home-mortgage-interest deduction.
23 The Form 1098 from IndyMac stated that Pinckney paid $36,362 of mortgage interest on the house in 2008, and the IRS concedes that monthly statements from IndyMac show Pinckney paid another $3,305 of mortgage interest in 2008. The amounts total $39,667. Pinckney wrote a check to IndyMac for $500 on May 18, 2008. Even if this check was a payment of home-mortgage interest, that payment might have been reflected in the $39,667 amount. Thus, even if the $500 check could support a finding that Pinckney paid $500 in home-mortgage interest, that would not mean he paid more than $39,667 in home-mortgage interest in 2008. See Wathen, T.C. Memo. 2021-100, at *24 & n.8; infra Part II.F.
102
[*102]
H. The determination in the Notice of Deficiency that Pinckney’s charitable-contribution deduction for 2008 is $4,840, rather than the $5,350 he claimed, is sustained.
Pinckney claimed a total charitable-contribution deduction of $5,350 for 2008. The Notice of Deficiency allowed only $4,840.
A taxpayer may deduct a charitable contribution paid within the tax year. § 170(a)(1).
Pinckney has the burden of persuasion as to the deduction. This is so because (1) the determination in the Notice of Deficiency to partially disallow the deduction is presumed correct, Cozzi, 88 T.C. at 443 & n.8, 444; (2) Pinckney has not introduced credible evidence of any payment of a charitable contribution for 2008, so as to meet the requirement of section 7491(a)(1); and (3) Pinckney has not shown that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2) have been met.
No testimony, records of the Daitech Account, or written record provided by Pinckney addresses the payment of any charitable contributions. Therefore Pinckney has not satisfied his burden of persuasion as to his entitlement to a charitable-contribution deduction. We sustain the IRS’s determination that the amount of the deduction is $4,840.
I. For 2008, Daitech was a disregarded entity rather than an S corporation, and therefore Pinckney’s self-employment income includes income with respect to Daitech’s activities.
The Federal Insurance Contributions Act (FICA) tax is imposed on the wages––defined as “all remuneration for employment”––of every individual with respect to employment, which, as relevant, is defined as “any service, of whatever nature, performed . . . by an employee for the person employing him.” §§ 3101(a) and (b), 3121(a) and (b). Half of the FICA tax is imposed on the employer, § 3111(a), (b), and half on the employee, § 3101(a); see also McDonald v. S. Farm Bureau Life Ins. Co., 291 F.3d 718, 721 (11th Cir. 2002). The earnings of self-employed persons are, as a result of the definitions of “wages” and “employment,” excepted from FICA. McDonald, 291 F.3d at 721.
In 1950, Congress enacted the self-employment tax as a counterpart to FICA for self-employed taxpayers. § 1401(a) and (b); see Social Security Act Amendments of 1950, ch. 809, § 208, 64 Stat. 477, 540–41 (1950); Hornaday v. Commissioner, 81 T.C. 830, 833–34 (1983).
103
[*103] The self-employment tax is imposed on self-employment income, which is defined as the net earnings from self-employment derived by the individual. §§ 1401(a) and (b), 1402(b), (h). “Net earnings from selfemployment” is defined generally as the gross income derived by an individual from any trade or business carried on by the individual minus the deductions attributable to the trade or business. § 1402(a). A trade or business for this purpose does not include the performance of service by an individual as an employee. § 1402(c)(2).
In 1958, Congress added subchapter S to the Internal Revenue Code of 1954. See Technical Amendments Act of 1958, Pub. L. No. 85- 866, § 64, 72 Stat. 1606, 1650 (1958). Under the current provisions of subchapter S (in the Internal Revenue Code of 1986), an S corporation is not subject to income tax, § 1363, and its income and deductions pass through to its shareholders, § 1366(a)–(c). Distributions by an S corporation with respect to its stock are generally not included in the gross income of its shareholders. § 1368(b) and (c); Cameron v. Commissioner, 105 T.C. 380, 383–84 (1995) (“The basic purpose of the earnings and profits account is to keep track of the amount of corporate funds that have not yet been taxed to shareholders. When a corporation elects pass-through treatment under subchapter S, its net income earned as an S corporation is taxed currently to the shareholders and thereafter is generally distributed tax-free.”), aff’d sub nom. Broadaway v. Commissioner, 111 F.3d 593 (8th Cir. 1997). Although an S corporation is not subject to income tax, the S corporation is subject to the FICA tax on wages paid to its employees as their employer. See, e.g., David E. Watson, P.C. v. United States, 668 F.3d 1008, 1017–18 (8th Cir. 2012); Joseph Radtke, S.C. v. United States, 895 F.2d 1196, 1197 (7th Cir. 1990) (per curiam); Veterinary Surgical Consultants, P.C. v. Commissioner, 117 T.C. 141, 145–46 (2001), aff’d sub nom. Yeagle Drywall Co. v. Commissioner, 54 F. App’x 100 (3d Cir. 2002). Thus, if the shareholder of the S corporation is an individual who works for the S corporation as an employee, the S corporation is liable for the employer share of the FICA tax on the shareholder’s wages. See Spicer Acct., Inc. v. United States, 918 F.2d 90, 93 (9th Cir. 1990); Nu-Look Design, Inc. v. Commissioner, 356 F.3d 290, 293–94 (3d Cir. 2004), aff’g T.C. Memo. 2003-52.
As explained before, net earnings from self-employment are equal generally to the gross income derived by an individual from a trade or business, minus deductions. § 1402(a). For individuals who own S corporations, it might be questioned how the income and deductions of the S corporation, which pass through to the shareholder for income
104
[*104] tax purposes, affect the individual’s net earnings from selfemployment for self-employment tax purposes. It is thought to be “well established” that the pass-through income and deductions are not calculated in the net earnings from self-employment in determining the shareholder’s self-employment tax. James S. Eustice, Joel D. Kuntz & John A. Bogdanski, Federal Income Taxation of S Corporations ¶ 7.13 (2025), Westlaw FTXSCORP (citing, inter alia, Ding v. Commissioner, 200 F.3d 587, 590 (9th Cir. 1999), aff’g T.C. Memo. 1997-435). However, once the S corporation distributes its earnings to a shareholder, this distribution triggers FICA tax liability if the shareholder is also an employee of the S corporation and if the distributions are disguised wages (i.e., payments for services of an employee). See Veterinary Surgical Consultants, P.C., 117 T.C. at 145–46; see also Taylor v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2009-235, 2009 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 238, at *12 (citing Veterinary Surgical Consultants, P.C., 117 T.C. at 144– 45).
An S corporation is defined, with respect to any tax year, as a small business corporation for which an election under section 1362(a) is in effect for that year. § 1361(a)(1). A small business corporation is defined as a U.S. corporation that, among other requirements, has 100 or fewer shareholders. § 1361(b)(1).
Section 1362(a)(1) provides generally that a small business corporation may elect to be an S corporation, with section 1362(a)(2) providing that an S corporation election is valid only if all shareholders on the day of the election consent to the election. Section 1362(b)(1) provides that an S corporation election may be made for any tax year (a) at any time during the preceding tax year or (b) at any time between the first day of the tax year and the 15th day of the third month of the tax year. Section 1362(b)(3) provides that if an S corporation election is made for a tax year after the 15th day of the third month of the tax year and on or before the 15th day of the third month of the following tax year, then such an election shall be treated as made for the following tax year. Section 1362(b)(5) provides that if no S corporation election is made for a tax year, or if an S corporation election is made after the deadline imposed by section 1362(b)(1), then the Treasury Department may treat the election as timely made for that tax year if the Treasury Department determines there was reasonable cause for the failure to timely make such an election. The manner of making an S corporation election under section 1362(a)(1) is by filing a completed Form 2553. Treas. Reg. § 1.1362-6(a)(2).
105
[*105] In December of 1996, the Treasury Department promulgated Treas. Reg. § 301.7701-3, entity-classification regulations. Under these regulations, which were effective as of January 1, 1997, an eligible entity is defined as a business entity that is not automatically classified as a corporation. Treas. Reg. § 301.7701-3(a) (current); see also Treas. Reg. § 301.7701-3(a) (1996) (consisting of same wording). An LLC is a business entity that is not automatically classified as a corporation. See Treas. Reg. § 301.7701-3(a), (b)(1) (flush text). Therefore, an LLC is an eligible entity. See TBL Licensing LLC v. Commissioner, 158 T.C. 1, 7 n.5 (2022), aff’d, 82 F.4th 12 (1st Cir. 2023). The entity-classification regulations provide that an eligible entity with a single owner can elect to be either (a) classified as an association (and thus a corporation) or (b) disregarded as an entity separate from its owner. Treas. Reg. §§ 301.7701-2(b)(2), 301.7701-3(a). A single-owner eligible entity that does not affirmatively elect to be classified as an association (and thus a corporation) is disregarded as an entity separate from its owner. Treas. Reg. § 301.7701-3(b)(1)(ii). An affirmative election is made under the entity-classification regulations by filing a Form 8832, Entity Classification Election, with the IRS. Id. (c)(1)(i). Treasury Regulation § 301.7701-3(c)(1)(iii) provides that the effective date of the election is the same as the date the Form 8832 is filed if no effective date is specified on the Form 8832. If an effective date is specified on the Form 8832, then the effective date of the election is the specified effective date, but the effective date cannot be more than 75 days before the date the Form 8832 is filed. Id.
Under the entity-classification regulations as promulgated in 1996, T.D. 8697, 1997-1 C.B. 215, 217, an LLC that wished to have S corporation status would need to make two separate elections. T.D. 9139, 2004-2 C.B. 495, 496 (explaining the 1996 regulation in preamble to subsequent regulation). First, it would need to elect to be classified as an association (and thus a corporation) by filing a Form 8832. T.D. 9139, 2004-2 C.B. at 496. Second and separately, it would need to elect to be an S corporation under section 1362(a) by filing a Form 2553. T.D. 9139, 2004-2 C.B. at 496.
Then, in 2004, Treasury Decision 9139 temporarily amended the entity-classification regulations to provide that an S corporation election by an eligible entity such as an LLC would be deemed an election to be treated as an association (and thus a corporation): “An eligible entity that timely elects to be an S corporation under section 1362(a)(1) is treated as having made an election . . . to be classified as an association . . . . [T]he deemed election to be classified as an association will apply
106
[*106] as of the effective date of the S corporation election . . . .” Temp. Treas. Reg. § 301.7701-3T(c)(1)(v)(C) (as amended by T.D. 9139, 2004-2 C.B. 495, 497). This rule applied to timely S corporation elections filed on or after July 20, 2004. Id. (c)(3). Furthermore, eligible entities that filed timely S elections before July 20, 2004, were allowed to rely on this rule. Id.
Alongside Treasury Decision 9139, in 2004 the IRS published Rev. Proc. 2004-48, 2004-2 C.B. 172. This revenue procedure set forth the procedure for an eligible entity seeking to be classified as an S corporation (through the filing of a Form 2553 that would be a deemed election to be classified as an association under the 2004 temporary regulation) to seek additional time to file the Form 2553. First, Rev. Proc. 2004-48 required the request for relief to be filed within six months after the due date for the Form 1120–S, excluding extensions.24 Rev. Proc. 2004-48, § 4.02, 2004-2 C.B. at 173. In addition, Rev. Proc. 2004- 48 required the request for relief to be filed within 18 months of the original due date of the S corporation election. See id. at preamble.
In 2005, the entity-classification regulations were amended to add Treasury Regulation § 301.7701-3(c)(1)(v)(C), a final regulation, which provides that “[a]n eligible entity that timely elects to be an S corporation under section 1362(a)(1) is treated as having made an election . . . to be classified as an association . . . . [T]he deemed election to be classified as an association will apply as of the effective date of the S corporation election . . . .” T.D. 9203, 2005-1 C.B. 1285, 1286. The 2005 provision was identical to the 2004 temporary provision. Thus, Revenue Procedure 2004-48 presumably governs requests to file a Form 2553 late under Treasury Regulation § 301.7701-3(c)(1)(v)(C).
Next, in 2007 the Treasury Department promulgated amendments to the entity-classification regulations. Under these amendments, an entity that is otherwise disregarded as an entity separate from its owner is nonetheless regarded as a corporation for purposes of FICA and other employment taxes. T.D. 9356, 2007-2 C.B. 675, 677–78 (amending, inter alia, Treas. Reg. § 301.7701-2(c)(2)(iv)(A), (B), (C), and (D) (examples (i) and (ii))). This rule was expressly made effective with respect to wages paid on or after January 1, 2009. Treas.
24 The Form 1120–S is due on or before the 15th day of the third month following the close of the tax year. Treas. Reg. § 1.6037-1(b).
107
[*107] Reg. § 301.7701-2(e)(5)(i) (flush text).25 Under the 2007 amendments, the owner of an entity that is disregarded as an entity separate from its owner is subject to the tax on self-employment income. Treas. Reg. § 301.7701-2(c)(2)(iv)(A), (C) (example (iii)) (2007), as amended by T.D. 10026, 90 Fed. Reg. 3003 (Jan. 14, 2025). There is no effective date provision that expressly governs this rule.26 Under this rule, the owner of an LLC is subject to the tax on self-employment income if the LLC has not elected to be a corporation for a tax year either through the filing of a Form 8832, see Treas. Reg. § 301.7701-3(c)(1)(i), or through the filing of a Form 2553 that would be deemed an election to be classified as an association (and thus a corporation), see id. (c)(1)(v)(C).
Pinckney takes the position that Daitech should be treated as an S corporation for the 2008 and 2009 tax years. Although Pinckney’s returns filed for these years reported Daitech’s income on Schedule Cs, Pinckney later submitted Form 1120–Ss for Daitech for these years. The Form 1120–Ss stated that the effective date of Daitech’s S corporation election was January 1, 2002. Pinckney also submitted amended personal income tax returns for 2008 and 2009 that calculated selfemployment taxes without including in his self-employment income the S corporation income reported on the Form 1120–Ss for Daitech.
In its Opening Brief, the IRS contends that Daitech is not an S corporation for tax years 2008 and 2009 because Pinckney first filed a Form 8832 for Daitech on April 23, 2012. The IRS contends that under Treasury Regulation § 301.7701-3(c)(1)(iii), the effective date specified on a Form 8832 cannot be more than 75 days before the date on which the election is filed. Thus, according to the IRS, the earliest possible effective date for classifying Daitech as an S corporation is February 8, 2012. As a result, the IRS contends, the election to treat Daitech as a corporation, made February 8, 2012, is invalid for the 2008 and 2009 tax years. The IRS cites no evidence for the proposition that the Form 8832 for Daitech was filed for Daitech on April 23, 2012. However, IRS records show that the IRS started to treat Daitech as an S corporation beginning with the 2012 year. The IRS’s opening brief does not discuss whether
25 For wages paid before this date, the sole member of an LLC that is a disregarded entity is considered to be the same taxpayer as the LLC itself. See Costello v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2016-184, at *10–12.
26 Neither party contends that this rule would not apply for the 2008 and 2009 tax years.
108
[*108] Pinckney filed a Form 2553 for Daitech or the significance of such a filing.
In his Opening Brief Pinckney contends that “[a]round March 2012, the Petitioner filed an election completing form [sic] 2553 electing Daitech Tax Services, LLC to be recognized by the IRS as an S-Corp. from 2002 to present.” On the basis of this contention, Pinckney argues that he is not liable for self-employment taxes as to Daitech’s activities for the 2008 and 2009 tax years. In response, the IRS’s Answering Brief still does not discuss the filing of the Form 2553. Instead, it contends that “it has been well established that an eligible entity may not elect its entity classification by filing any particular tax return it wishes; it must do so by filing Form 8832,” and under Treasury Regulation § 301.7701-3(c)(1)(iii), “[e]ven assuming that Pinckney did file a Form 8832 on March 1, 2012, the earlier possible effective date of the entity classification for Daitech would be December 17, 2011.” The IRS’s Answering Brief expressly assumes that a Form 8832 is the sole mechanism for an “election to treat Daitech as an S-Corporation for federal tax purposes.”
Before Temporary Treasury Regulation § 301.7701-3T(c)(1)(v)(C) was promulgated in 2004, Pinckney would have had to file both Forms 8832 and 2553 to have Daitech, an LLC, classified as an S Corporation. See T.D. 9139, 2004-2 C.B. at 496 (“A taxpayer whose default classification is a . . . disregarded entity may seek to be classified as an S corporation. . . . [T]he taxpayer must elect to be classified as an association under § 301.7701-3(c)(1)(i) by filing Form 8832 and must elect to be an S corporation under section 1362(a) by filing Form 2553, ‘Election by a Small Business Corporation’ . . . , [r]equiring eligible entities to file two elections in order to be classified as S corporations . . . .”). But after the 2004 regulation, Pinckney needed only to file a Form 2553 for Daitech to be treated as an S corporation. Temp. Treas. Reg. § 301.7701-3T(c)(1)(v)(C); Treas. Reg. § 301.7701-3(c)(1)(v)(C). Pinckney alleges he filed a Form 2553, and the IRS has not specifically denied that he filed it. That he filed a Form 2553 in 2012 is not inconsistent with the IRS’s records, which show that starting with the 2012 tax year, it considered Daitech to be an S corporation. We consider the IRS to have conceded that Pinckney filed a Form 2553 for Daitech during the 2012 calendar year.
However, the filing of a Form 2553 during the 2012 calendar year is insufficient for Daitech to be treated as an S corporation for the 2008 tax year. The problem is that the Form 2553 was untimely filed. For
109
[*109] Daitech to be an S corporation for the 2008 tax year, Pinckney would have had to file his Form 2553 between January 1, 2007, and March 17, 2008. See §§ 1362(b)(1), 7503.27 The Form 2553 filing, which concededly occurred in 2012, is therefore not effective as to the 2008 tax year.28
We thus hold that Daitech was not an S corporation for the 2008 tax year.29
The amount of the self-employment tax liability of Pinckney will be affected by our holding that the gross receipts of Daitech are $376,712, rather than $382,243, as determined by the Notice of Deficiency. We leave it to the parties to recompute the liability under Rule 155.
27 Under section 1362(b)(1), the last day for Daitech to file a Form 2553 was March 15, 2008. However, March 15, 2008, was a Saturday. Therefore, under section 7503, a Form 2553 filed on Monday, March 17, 2008, would be timely.
28 There is no evidence that Pinckney filed for an extension of the March 17, 2008, deadline under Revenue Procedure 2004-48. Even had he filed a request for an extension in 2012 (i.e., even had he filed a request for an extension at the same time the IRS conceded he had actually filed a Form 2553), such a request for an extension would have been untimely under Revenue Procedure 2004-48. Under Revenue Procedure 2004-48, § 4.02, 2004-2 C.B. at 173, the request for the extension had to be filed six months from the due date of the Form 1120–S. The Form 1120–S for tax year 2008 was due Sunday, March 15, 2009, a deadline which for these purposes is determined without accounting for the extra days allowed for a deadline falling on a weekend day. See § 7503; Zaimes v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2023-121, at *6; Treas. Reg. § 1.6037-1(b). Six months from this Form 1120–S due date was September 15, 2009. A request for an extension filed in 2012 would have missed that September 15, 2009, deadline. Furthermore, the request for an extension had to be filed no later than 18 months from the original due date of the S corporation election, an election which is made on Form 2553. Rev. Proc. 2004-48, 2004-2 C.B. 172 at preamble. The Form 2553 for tax year 2008 was due Saturday, March 15, 2008, a deadline which for these purposes is determined without accounting for the extra days allowed for a deadline falling on a weekend day. See Zaimes, T.C. Memo. 2023-121, at *6; § 1362(b)(1); § 7503. Eighteen months after March 15, 2008, was September 15, 2009. A request for an extension filed in 2012 would have missed this September 15, 2009, deadline.
29 We draw the same conclusion as to the 2009 tax year. See infra Part II.I.
110
[*110]
II. We sustain all the adjustments underlying the 2009 deficiency determination, except for the adjustment of Daitech’s businessexpense deductions to $63,199, which we determine to instead be $66,963, and except for the adjustment to self-employment tax, the amount of which must account for our findings that Daitech’s business-expense deductions are greater than the amount determined in the Notice of Deficiency.
A. Pinckney failed to report $20 of other income.
Applying the legal principles discussed supra Part I.A, we conclude that Pinckney bears the burden of persuasion with respect to the alleged $20 of other income.
First, we hold that the IRS has employed a reasonable method of determining that Pinckney earned the unreported income. See Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d at 763–64. The IRS introduced its records, which, as Baumgartner explained in her testimony, indicate that a third party, E*Trade Clearing, LLC, issued a Form 1099–MISC to Pinckney stating that it had paid him $20 of income. The IRS has thus shown it employed a reasonable method for determining income. See Page v. Commissioner, 58 F.3d 1342, 1347 (8th Cir. 1995), aff’g T.C. Memo. 1993-398; Ernle v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2010-237, 2010 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 274, at *6–7.
Second, assuming––without deciding––that the “minimal evidentiary showing” is required here, we hold that the IRS has established a minimal evidentiary showing connecting Pinckney with the activity producing the income. See, e.g., Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d at 763–64. As explained above, the IRS introduced its records showing that a third party had issued an information return stating that it had paid income to Pinckney. This information return satisfies the minimal evidentiary showing. See Rodriguez, 2009 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 93, at *4; Oman, 2010 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 313, at *14–16. Additionally, Daitech’s bank statements show that Pinckney had a transactional relationship with E*Trade Clearing, LLC. This also satisfies the minimal evidentiary showing.
Third, we hold that the IRS is not subject to the additional evidentiary burden imposed by section 6201(d). The amount in question is an item of income reported on an information return within the meaning of section 6201(d). Section 6201(d) would impose an additional
111
[*111] evidentiary burden on the IRS had Pinckney asserted a reasonable dispute about the information return. Pinckney did not do so.
Fourth, we hold that section 7491(a)(1) does not impose the burden of persuasion on the IRS with respect to the $20 adjustment to income. Section 7491(a)(1) provides that if, in any court proceeding, the taxpayer introduces credible evidence with respect to any factual issue, the IRS shall have the burden of proof with respect to such issue if the conditions of section 7491(a)(2) are met. Pinckney has introduced no evidence, credible or not, as to whether he earned the alleged income. Nor has Pinckney shown that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2) are met. For example, he has not shown that he maintained all required records that would reflect the income (a condition of section 7491(a)(2) imposed by section 7491(a)(2)(B)).
Having established that Pinckney has the burden of persuasion with respect to the alleged $20 of income, we now address whether he has satisfied this burden. The burden of persuasion is satisfied if the taxpayer shows that, on the basis of the evidence, the fact is more probable than not. Merkel, 109 T.C. at 476 (citing 2 McCormick on Evidence, supra, § 339, at 439). No evidence was adduced by Pinckney to indicate that he did not earn the alleged income. We find that Pinckney earned $20 of unreported income.
B. Pinckney failed to report $445 of capital-gain income.
Applying the legal principles discussed supra Part I.A, we conclude that Pinckney bears the burden of persuasion with respect to $445 of capital-gain income.
First, we hold that the IRS has employed a reasonable method of determining that Pinckney earned the unreported income. See Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d at 763–64. The IRS introduced its records which, as Baumgartner explained in her testimony, indicate that a third party, E*Trade Clearing, LLC, issued a Form 1099–B to Pinckney stating that he had $445 of capital-gain income. The IRS has thus shown it employed a reasonable method for determining income. See Page v. Commissioner, 58 F.3d at 1347; Ernle, 2010 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 274, at *6–7.
Second, assuming––without deciding––that the “minimal evidentiary showing” is required here, we hold that the IRS has established a minimal evidentiary showing connecting Pinckney with
112
[*112] the activity producing the income. See, e.g., Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d at 763–64. As explained above, the IRS introduced its records showing that a third party (E*Trade Clearing, LLC) had issued an information return stating that Pinckney had capital-gain income. This information return satisfies the minimal evidentiary showing. See Hardy v. Commissioner, 181 F.3d at 1004–05; Rodriguez, 2009 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 93, at *4. Additionally, Daitech’s bank statements show that Pinckney had a transactional relationship with E*Trade Clearing, LLC. This also satisfies the minimal evidentiary showing.
Third, we hold that the IRS is not subject to the additional evidentiary burden imposed by section 6201(d). The amount in question is an item of income reported on an information return within the meaning of section 6201(d). Section 6201(d) would impose an additional evidentiary burden on the IRS had Pinckney asserted a reasonable dispute about the information return. Pinckney has not done so.
Fourth, we hold that section 7491(a)(1) does not impose the burden of persuasion on the IRS with respect to the capital gain. Section 7491(a)(1) provides that if, in any court proceeding, the taxpayer introduces credible evidence with respect to any factual issue, the IRS shall have the burden of proof with respect to that issue, provided that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2) are met. Pinckney has introduced no evidence, credible or not, as to whether he had the alleged capital gain. Nor has Pinckney shown that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2) are met. For example, he has not shown that he maintained all required records that would reflect the capital-gain income (a condition imposed by section 7491(a)(2)(B)).
Having established that Pinckney has the burden of persuasion with respect to the alleged capital-gain income, we now address whether he has satisfied this burden. The burden of persuasion is satisfied if the taxpayer shows that, on the basis of the evidence, the fact is more probable than not. Merkel, 109 T.C. at 476 (citing 2 McCormick on Evidence, supra, § 339, at 439). No evidence was adduced by Pinckney to indicate that he did not have the alleged capital gain. We find that Pinckney realized $445 of capital-gain income for 2009.
113
[*113]
C. Pinckney failed to report $292 of dividend income.
Applying the legal principles discussed above, see supra Part I.A, we conclude that Pinckney bears the burden of persuasion with respect to the $292 of dividend income.
First, we hold that the IRS has employed a reasonable method of determining that Pinckney earned the dividend income. See Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d at 763–64. The IRS introduced its records which, as Baumgartner explained in her testimony, indicate that a third party, E*Trade Clearing, LLC, issued a Form 1099–DIV to Pinckney stating that he had $292 of dividend income. The IRS has thus shown it employed a reasonable method for determining income. See Page v. Commissioner, 58 F.3d at 1347; Ernle, 2010 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 274, at *6–7.
Second, assuming––without deciding––that the “minimal evidentiary showing” is required here, we hold that the IRS has established a minimal evidentiary showing connecting Pinckney with the activity producing the dividend income. See, e.g., Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d at 763–64. As explained above, the IRS introduced its records showing that a third party (E*Trade Clearing, LLC) had issued an information return stating that Pinckney had the dividend income. This information return satisfies the minimal evidentiary showing. See Hardy v. Commissioner, 181 F.3d at 1004–05; Rodriguez, 2009 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 93, at *4; Oman, 2010 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 313, at *14–16. Additionally, Daitech’s bank statements show that Pinckney had a transactional relationship with E*Trade Clearing, LLC. This also satisfies the minimal evidentiary showing.
Third, we hold that the IRS is not subject to the additional evidentiary burden imposed by section 6201(d). The dividend is an item of income reported on an information return within the meaning of section 6201(d). Section 6201(d) would impose an additional evidentiary burden on the IRS had Pinckney asserted a reasonable dispute about the information return. Pinckney has not done so.
Fourth, we hold that section 7491(a)(1) does not impose the burden of persuasion on the IRS with respect to the dividend. Section 7491(a)(1) provides that if, in any court proceeding, the taxpayer introduces credible evidence with respect to any factual issue, the IRS shall have the burden of proof with respect to that issue, provided that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2) are met. Pinckney has introduced no
114
[*114] evidence, credible or not, as to whether he had the alleged dividend. Nor has Pinckney shown that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2) are met. For example, he has not shown that he maintained all required records that would reflect the dividend (a condition of section 7491(a)(2) imposed by section 7491(a)(2)(B)).
Having established that Pinckney has the burden of persuasion with respect to the alleged dividend, we now address whether he has satisfied this burden. The burden of persuasion is satisfied if the taxpayer shows that, on the basis of the evidence, the fact is more probable than not. Merkel, 109 T.C. at 476 (citing 2 McCormick on Evidence, supra, § 339, at 439). Pinckney adduced no evidence to indicate that he did not receive the dividend. We find that Pinckney received $292 of taxable dividends for 2009.
D. We sustain the determination in the Notice of Deficiency that the amount of gross receipts and other income for Daitech is $340,765, rather than the $143,080 reported on Pinckney’s return.
Pinckney’s 2009 return reported that Daitech’s gross receipts were $5,530 and that its other income was $137,550, for a total gross income (before reduction for cost of goods sold) of $143,080. The Notice of Deficiency, which was dated February 6, 2019, determined that the amount was $340,765. Baumgartner took the view that the amount was $416,171, on the basis of her bank-deposit analysis performed on bank records that were acquired by the IRS on October 25, 2021. That analysis concluded that there were taxable deposits into the Daitech Account of $416,171. The IRS litigating position, consistent with the Notice of Deficiency, is that the amount is $340,765.
As with the bank-deposit analysis for 2008, the IRS has the burden of proving that a particular deposit came from a taxable source because the bank-deposit analysis is properly considered a new matter. See supra Part I.C. The 2018 30-day letter preceded the Notice of Deficiency by nine months but contained the same determination as the Notice of Deficiency as to the gross income of Daitech (before reduction by the cost of goods sold) for tax year 2009: $340,765. The 2018 30-day letter makes no mention of any bank-deposit analysis. Instead, it made the following statement as to the source of the determination: “Per [the] indictment: 340765.25.” The Notice of Deficiency made the same determination amount (i.e., $340,765) but does not mention the indictment or a bank-deposit analysis. Furthermore, the only bank-
115
[*115] deposit analysis in the record was performed by Baumgartner on bank records obtained by the IRS after the mailing of the Notice of Deficiency. We therefore conclude that the burden of persuasion rests on the IRS.
Assuming––without deciding––that the IRS is also required to establish a “minimal evidentiary showing” connecting Pinckney with the activity generating the alleged income, see, e.g., Williams v. Commissioner, 999 F.2d at 763–64, we hold that this showing was met. The activity generating the alleged income is the Daitech business, and the record shows that Pinckney owned and operated Daitech.
We now consider whether the IRS has satisfied its burden of persuasion.
