income Republic TRS Prep $401,884 Tax Prep Checks 9,213 Cash Deposit 5,075 Non-Taxable 61,419 Total $416,171 $61,419
We note that the table above and the main spreadsheet reflect an arithmetic error Baumgartner made in calculating the sum of Tax Prep Checks deposits made on April 15, 2009. On this day there were five deposits considered by Baumgartner to be in the Tax Prep Checks category: (1) “Enails $360”; (2) “Lorick $200”; (3) “Kelly $175”; (4) “Burrell $100”; and (5) “Posey [sic] $180.” The actual sum of these amounts is $1,015. Baumgartner’s spreadsheet reflects a sum of $1,005,
120
[*120] which is $10 less than the actual sum. As a result, the $9,213 amount shown in the table above should have been $9,223 and the $416,171 amount in the table above should have been $416,181. In addition, the $416,171 amount in the table above reflects an arithmetic error of $1; the $416,171 amount should have been $416,172. If one corrects for both the $10 error and the $1 error, the $416,171 amount in the table should have been $416,182. Though we make this correction in this discussion, however, it would not have been necessary to make the two corrections because even the lower uncorrected total of taxable deposits—$416,171—exceeds the total of gross receipts and other income determined in the Notice of Deficiency.
Each deposit in Baumgartner’s first category of deposits, “Republic TRS Prep,” was described as “Republic TRS Prep” on the relevant bank statement. Baumgartner concluded that these deposits were income from Daitech’s operations as a tax-return preparer. We find that a preponderance of the evidence supports Baumgartner’s classification of the deposits as taxable.
The second category of deposits, “Tax Prep,” consisted of deposits that had the following characteristics: (1) the bank statement described the deposit as a check deposit, rather than a direct deposit; (2) the Daitech Account records contained a copy of a check that corresponded to the deposit and (a) the check was written by a Daitech customer, and (b) the check had a memo line that contained a description similar to “Tax Prep” or “taxes.” Baumgartner concluded that these deposits, too, were income. We find that a preponderance of the evidence supports Baumgartner’s classification of the deposits as taxable.
The third category of deposits, “Cash,” consists of all deposits for which the bank records included a First Community Bank printout titled “Cash In Ticket” prepared by a bank teller. A “Cash In Ticket” was prepared for all deposits made in cash.30 Baumgartner testified that the cash deposits were payments for services, because of the nature of the Daitech business, and thus, that these deposits were income. We find that a preponderance of the evidence supports Baumgartner’s classification of these deposits as taxable.
30 For each cash deposit, the bank records contain not only a Cash In Ticket, but also a deposit slip. The deposit slip showed the total amount of cash deposited for each instance in which cash was deposited and also showed the total amount of all checks (if any) that were deposited at the same time the cash was deposited.
121
[*121] For deposits in the fourth category, “non-taxable,” Baumgartner concluded that the deposits were not includable in Pinckney’s gross income. The IRS on brief does not contest this conclusion. Therefore, we need not resolve whether the deposits are includable in Pinckney’s gross income.
Our conclusions, therefore, are as follows:
Daitech Account––2009
Includable
in gross
Not includable
income
in gross
income Republic TRS Prep $401,884 Tax Prep Checks 9,223 Cash Deposit 5,075 Non-Taxable $61,419 Total $416,182 $61,419
In our view of record, $416,182 of the deposits into the Daitech Account is includable in gross income for 2009. This amount exceeds the $340,765 total of deposits determined by the Notice of Deficiency as includable in gross income for 2009. Therefore, we sustain the determination of the Notice of Deficiency.
In forming our views, we are unpersuaded by various reasons Pinckney gave why we should totally reject Baumgartner’s use of the bank-deposits method (i.e., that Baumgartner’s testimony was coached, that Baumgartner did not understand the “net worth of proof” method, and that Baumgartner failed to consider the deductibility of payments made from the Daitech Account). We discussed these reasons above. See supra Part I.C.
E. Daitech’s cost of goods sold is zero, not $30,667 as reported on the Schedule C.
Pinckney reported on the Schedule C for Daitech that Daitech’s cost of goods sold was $30,667. The Notice of Deficiency determined that this amount was zero.
Taxable income is equal to gross income minus deductions, § 63(a), one type of which is the deduction under section 162(a) for business expenses. When a taxpayer earns gross income from selling goods, the gross income from those sales is equal to the receipts from the
122
[*122] sales (total sales) minus the cost of goods sold. § 61(a)(2); Treas. Reg. § 1.61-3(a). Cost of goods sold is calculated as the sum of the cost of beginning inventory and the cost of purchases (and other acquisition or production costs) during the tax year less the cost of ending inventory. Huffman, 126 T.C. at 324; see also Treas. Reg. §§ 1.162-1(a), 1.446- 1(a)(4)(i), 1.471-1.
Pinckney has the burden of persuasion as to the amount of costof-goods sold. This is because (1) the determination in the Notice of Deficiency that amount is zero is presumed correct, Cozzi, 88 T.C. 443 & n.8, 444; (2) Pinckney has not introduced credible evidence of the amount that would satisfy the requirement of section 7491(a)(1); and (3) Pinckney has not shown that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2) have been met.
The record contains no evidence that Daitech sold any goods. The record shows instead that Daitech was in the business of filing tax returns, which is a service. Therefore, Pinckney has not satisfied his burden of persuasion as to the cost of goods sold.
F. Daitech’s business-expense deductions are $66,963 rather than the $63,199 determined in the Notice of Deficiency.
Pinckney’s return for 2009 includes a Schedule C for Daitech. On this Schedule C, he claimed $60,202 of business-expense deductions, divided into various categories corresponding to line items on the Schedule C. The Notice of Deficiency allowed $63,199 of businessexpense deductions for Daitech. The Notice of Deficiency divided these allowed deductions into various categories corresponding to the line items on the Schedule C.
Section 162(a) allows a taxpayer to deduct all ordinary and necessary expenses incurred in carrying on a trade or business. An expense is “necessary” if it is “appropriate and helpful” in the taxpayer’s business, but it need not be essential. Commissioner v. Tellier, 383 U.S. at 689 (citing Welch v. Helvering, 290 U.S. at 113). A taxpayer is required to maintain sufficient records to establish the amounts of allowable deductions and to enable the IRS to determine his or her correct tax liability. § 6001; Treas. Reg. § 1.6001-1(a). Under the Cohan rule, if a taxpayer establishes that he or she paid a deductible expense but is unable to substantiate the precise amount, a court may estimate the amount of the allowable deduction, bearing heavily against the taxpayer whose inexactitude is of his or her own making. Cohan v.
123
[*123] Commissioner, 39 F.2d at 543–44. For a court to estimate the amount of the allowable deduction under the Cohan rule, there must be sufficient evidence in the record to provide a basis for such an estimate. Vanicek, 85 T.C. at 742–43. Section 274(d) supersedes the Cohan rule with respect to some deductions, imposing strict substantiation requirements for deductions related to travel, meals, and lodging while away from home, entertainment, gifts, and “listed property” (a term defined in section 280F(d)(4) to include passenger automobiles). Sanford, 50 T.C. at 827–28.
Section 280A(a) provides that no deductions are allowed with respect to a dwelling unit used as the taxpayer’s residence. There are exceptions to this rule, such as for an expense allocable to a portion of a dwelling unit used exclusively or on a regular basis as a place of business used by clients in meeting or dealing with the taxpayer in the normal course of business. § 280A(c)(1)(B).
For the same reasons given with respect to the 2008 Schedule C for Daitech, see supra Part I.F, Pinckney has the burden of persuasion as to the deductions claimed on the 2009 Schedule C for Daitech. To show an error by the determination in the Notice of Deficiency that the total business-expense deductions of Daitech are $63,199, Pinckney must prove that the total allowable business-expense deductions regarding Daitech exceed $63,199.
To evaluate whether Pinckney has proven that Daitech had more than $63,199 in business-expense deductions for 2009, we consult the sole source of documentary evidence potentially related to those deductions. This source is the bank records regarding the Daitech Account. The table below sets forth all the information from the bank records regarding payments from the Daitech Account:

149
[*149] In addition to the Daitech Account bank records, Pinckney’s testimony is a source of information we consider in our determination of the amount of the deductions.
For most of the payments from the Daitech Account, neither the bank records nor Pinckney’s testimony shows the connection between the payment and Daitech. In this abundant class of payments, we include the following types of payments:
Payments for which we know from the bank records what
product and service was purchased, but for which there is no indication in the trial record how the product and service related to Daitech. For example, the bank records show that on February 11, 2009, Pinckney paid $463 to State Farm Insurance. This payment likely was for insurance coverage. But we do not have an indication in the trial record that the insurance coverage related to Daitech.
Payments for which Pinckney’s testimony was merely
conclusory as to business purpose. For example, when IRS counsel asked Pinckney whether the $127 payment to ADT Security Services on Plexus on May 13, 2009, was a business expense, Pinckney replied with, “that’s the business.”
Payments about which Pinckney gave no testimony at all.
Pinckney testified about the payments from the Daitech Account only when asked about them by IRS counsel. For payments about which IRS counsel asked no question, there is no testimony in the record. Furthermore, Pinckney refused to answer questions by IRS counsel about some payments on Fifth Amendment grounds. For these payments, too, there is no testimony in the trial record.
We find that no payments in this class were the ordinary and necessary expenses of carrying on the business of Daitech. The mere fact that the expenses were paid out of the Daitech Account is insufficient to carry Pinckney’s burden of proving that they are business expenses. It appears that it was Pinckney alone who decided whether to pay the expenses out of the Daitech Account. We discerned little indication that Pinckney distinguished between personal expenses and business expenses when doing so. That the Daitech Account was not limited to business expenses is suggested by the sheer number of payments for clothing, entertainment, and food.
150
[*150] We now discuss the second class of payments, which consists of all payments from the Daitech Account except for those in the first class. These are payments for which there is some colorable indication, either from documents or testimony, that they are connected to Daitech. An exhaustive list of such payments follows:
A January 6, 2009,31 check was written to “Urban Radio of SC”
for $810. The “For” line of the check stated that the payment was for a “Radio Commercial.” Although Pinckney could have made a payment to Urban Radio of South Carolina for personal purposes, we find on a preponderance of the evidence that this $810 payment is a business expense of Daitech.
A January 21, 2009, check was written to an “Ascot
Homeowners Association” for $810. Pinckney stated that this payment was for “HOA dues” and was “[f]or business use of a home.” Pinckney’s testimony that the payment related to his house does not prove his entitlement to a business-expense deduction. Section 280A(a) provides that no deduction is allowed an individual with respect to the use of a dwelling unit which is used by the taxpayer during the taxable year as a residence. Although there are exceptions, for example, for an expense allocable to a portion of the dwelling unit which is exclusively used on a regular basis as a place of business of the taxpayer, § 280A(c)(1)(B), Pinckney has not proven that a portion of his house was used exclusively on a regular basis as a place of business. That is because Pinckney has not shown that he used any portion of his house exclusively on a regular basis as a place of business that is used by clients in meeting or dealing with him. Nor has Pinckney proven that any other exception to section 280A(a) is applicable. Therefore, an expense related to his house is not deductible by operation of section 280A(a).
A January 21, 2009, check written to “Richland County
Treasury” for $4,801.58. Pinckney testified that the check was for taxes “for” his “house” and further testified that the check was “[f]or business use for my home.” Recall that on his Schedule A for 2009 Pinckney claimed a $4,107 deduction for
31 As in the 2008 analysis, these dates refer to the date the check was written, rather than the date the check was debited. For example, the “Radio Commercial” check (no. 777) was debited on January 28, 2009.
151
[*151]
real-estate taxes. Section 164(a)(1) allows a deduction for certain taxes, including “State and local . . . real property taxes.” A section 164(a)(1) deduction is an itemized deduction reportable on Schedule A. §§ 63(d), 62(a); see Treas. Reg. § 1.62-1(c)(1) (incorporating Temp. Treas. Reg. § 1.62- 1T(c)(1)); see also, e.g., Hawthorne v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 1999-31, 1999 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 31, at *20 n.6. Section 280A(a) disallows deductions with respect to a taxpayer’s use of a dwelling unit as a residence, but section 280A(b) provides that section 280A(a) does not apply to any deduction allowable without regard to its connection with the taxpayer’s trade or business. The deduction under section 164(a)(1) is such a deduction. See Tschetter v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2003-326, 2003 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 325, at *24. Therefore, section 280A(a) does not disallow deductions for real-estate taxes under section 164(a)(1). § 280A(b); Didonato v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2013-11, at *96. Section 162(a) allows a deduction for ordinary and necessary expenses paid during the taxable year in carrying on a trade or business. Real estate taxes can be deducted as a business expense under section 162 rather than section 164(a)(1), with those section 162 real estate tax deductions to be reported on Schedule C. See Coffman v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2000-7, 2000 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 7, at *15 (allowing a $406 Schedule C deduction for real estate taxes and interest). Section 280A(c)(1)(B) provides that section 280A(a) does not apply to any item to the extent such item is allocable to a portion of the dwelling unit that is used exclusively on a regular basis as a place of business that is used by clients in dealing with the taxpayer. Therefore, section 280A(a) does not disallow deductions for real estate taxes that are allocable to the portion of a dwelling unit used exclusively on a regular basis as a place of business that is used by clients in dealing with the taxpayer. See, e.g., Conrad v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2023-100, at *58. Here, however, even if the payment to Richland County is a payment for real-estate taxes on Pinckney’s house, as Pinckney claims, the payment is not deductible under section 162(a). That is so because Pinckney has not shown that he used any portion of his house exclusively on a regular basis as a place of business that is used by clients in meeting or dealing with him. Therefore, he has not shown that the section 280A(c)(1)(B) exception is applicable to the payment. Nor has he shown that any other
152
[*152]
exception in section 280A(a) is applicable. If the payment to Richland County is of real-estate taxes, then Pinckney is allowed an itemized Schedule A deduction of $4,801. However, it may be that he has already been allowed a deduction of this amount in two ways. First, he claimed a deduction of $4,107.00 for real-estate taxes on his Schedule A, which the Notice of Deficiency did not disallow. This may have included a portion of the $4,801 payment. Second, the Notice of Deficiency allowed $63,199.00 of Schedule C deductions, which may have included the $4,801.00 payment.32 In conclusion, the $4,801 payment, if for real estate taxes, would be an itemized deduction, but the record does not show that the amount was not already included in deductible amounts allowed by the Notice of Deficiency. See Wathen, T.C. Memo. 2021-100, at *24 & n.8.
A January 28, 2009, check was written to “Discover Business
Card” for $3,517. The word “Business” in the name of the payee might suggest that the payment was for a credit card related to the Daitech business. This slight indication is insufficient to show that the $3,517 payment is a business expense of Daitech. We do not know what was purchased with this credit card. None of the credit card statements for this credit card were made a part of the record. We have little reliable indication that the purchases with the credit card were related to Daitech’s business. And ultimately, the purchases with the business card could be personal expenses, and given that Pinckney has the burden of proof, we conclude they were indeed personal purchases. For the same reasons, we hold that the following other checks written to “Discover Business Card” are nondeductible: April 21, 2009 ($833); May 22, 2009 ($697); June 25, 2009 ($500); and November 27, 2009 ($250).

Credit” for $887. Although the word “Business” might suggest that the payment related to the Daitech business, this is insufficient to convince us that the $887 payment was a business expense of Daitech.

Therefore, an expense related to his house is not deductible by operation of section 280A(a).

Pinckney cannot be his own employee. Thus, a payment by Daitech to benefit of its sole proprietor, Pinckney, is nondeductible.

Pinckney testified that the payment “was for business for those oil changes and all that good stuff.” Without any specifics about what car was used for the Daitech business, and without information about Pinckney’s other vehicles, we are unable to conclude on a preponderance of the evidence that the expense was for a business vehicle.
There are several payments from the Daitech Account that are
designated as for payroll in one of four ways. First, the bank statement could describe the payment with the word “Payroll.” Second, the bank statement could describe the payment with the word “Wages.” Third, the bank statement could describe the payment with the words “Payroll Service Fee.” Fourth, a check via which the payment was made could have the notation that it was for “Payroll.” In total, these payrollassociated payments equal $48,742. (This total does not include a $9,000 payment to Eric Pinckney of “Wages Commissioner” on October 29, 2009, because a taxpayer
160
[*160]
cannot deduct wages he paid to himself.) We conclude that payments of $48,742 from the Daitech Account in 2009 are deductible as business expenses of Daitech as payroll and payroll-related expenses.
There are several payments by check to “D&H Investments”
and to “Tobin Company.” Many of the checks for 2008 and 2009 have memo lines noting that the payments are for office rent or for office utilities. We surmise that all of the payments to D&H Investments and Tobin Company are payments of rent or utilities on Daitech offices. We conclude that these payments are deductible. For 2009, the total payments to D&H Investments were $8,400. The total payments to Tobin Company were $8,446. We hold that the sum of these amounts (i.e., $16,846 = $8,400 + $8,446) is deductible as a business expense of Daitech.
In summary, we have divided the payments from the Daitech Account during 2009 into two classes. The second class of payments, which are enumerated payment by payment in the discussion above, consists of those payments for which the evidence arguably points to a connection to the operation of Daitech. As to this second class of payments, we conclude that $66,963 of the payments are deductible. (This is the sum of $810, $77, $488, $48,742, $8,400, and $8,446.) The first class of payments, which are definitionally all payments from the Daitech Account except for the second class of payments, is not deductible.
The deductible business expenses of Daitech are $66,963, which is greater than the $63,199 amount determined in the Notice of Deficiency.
G. The Notice of Deficiency did not err in disallowing the $5,000 deduction for self-employed health insurance.
Employees and some self-employed individuals are allowed to deduct “the amount paid during the taxable year for insurance which constitutes medical care.” § 162(l)(1); see § 401(c)(1). For tax year 2009 Pinckney claimed a deduction for health insurance of $5,000. The IRS disallowed this deduction in full.
Pinckney has the burden of persuasion as to the deduction. This is so because (1) the determination in the Notice of Deficiency to disallow the deduction is presumed correct, Cozzi, 88 T.C. at 443 & n.8, 444;
161
[*161] (2) Pinckney has not introduced credible evidence of any payment for health insurance during 2009, so as to meet the requirement of section 7491(a)(1); and (3) Pinckney has not shown that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2)(B) have been met.
Pinckney failed to prove by a preponderance of the evidence that he is entitled to the deduction. At trial, Pinckney did not provide any records to the Court which would substantiate that he paid any healthinsurance costs. None of the Daitech bank records for 2009 show that he made any payments to an insurance company. We hold that Pinckney cannot deduct health-insurance expenses.
H. The Notice of Deficiency did not err in determining that Pinckney’s charitable contribution deduction is $3,850, rather than $4,350 as claimed on his return.
Pinckney claimed charitable-contribution deductions of $4,350 for 2008. The Notice of Deficiency allowed only $3,850.
A taxpayer may deduct a charitable contribution paid within the tax year. § 170(a)(1).
Pinckney has the burden of persuasion as to the deduction. This is so because (1) the determination in the Notice of Deficiency to partially disallow the deduction is presumed correct, Cozzi, 88 T.C. at 443 & n.8, 444; (2) Pinckney has not introduced credible evidence of any payment of a charitable contribution for 2009 so as to meet the requirement of section 7491(a)(1); and (3) Pinckney has not shown that the conditions of section 7491(a)(2)(B) have been met.
No testimony, records of the Daitech Account, or written record provided by Pinckney addresses the payment of charitable contributions. Therefore Pinckney has not satisfied his burden of persuasion as to his entitlement to charitable-contribution deductions. We sustain the IRS’s determination that the amount of the deduction is $3,850.
I. For 2009, Daitech was a disregarded entity rather than an S corporation, and therefore Pinckney’s self-employment income includes income with respect to Daitech’s activities.
As explained supra Part I.I, the IRS has conceded that Pinckney filed a Form 2553 for Daitech during the 2012 calendar year. For Daitech to be an S corporation for the 2009 tax year, without extension,
162
[*162] Pinckney would have had to file the Form 2553 between January 1, 2008, and March 15, 2009. The Form 2553 filing, which, concededly, occurred in 2012, is therefore not effective as to the 2009 tax.34
We thus hold that Daitech was not an S corporation for the 2009 tax year.
The amount of the self-employment tax liability of Pinckney will be affected by our holding that the business-expense deductions of Daitech are $66,963, rather than $63,199, as determined by the Notice of Deficiency. We leave it to the parties to recompute the liability under Rule 155.
J. The deficiency for 2009 is unaffected by Pinckney’s submission of an amended return for 2009.
Pinckney submitted an amended return for 2009 on or after April 8, 2012. On that return, Pinckney reported that his tax liability was $3,202. Pinckney contends that the amended return was accepted by the IRS (or that if it was not accepted, it should have been accepted) and that therefore his correct tax liability for 2009 is $3,202. Whether the IRS accepts an amended return is a matter of agency discretion and does not affect the taxpayer’s correct tax liability. See Koch v. Alexander, 561 F.2d 1115, 1117 (4th Cir. 1977) (per curiam) (citing, inter alia, Klinghamer v. Brodrick, 242 F.2d 563 (10th Cir. 1957)); Willis v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 1992-332, 1992 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 337, at *3 (citing Miskovsky v. United States, 414 F.2d 954, 955 (3d Cir. 1969)); McCabe v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 1983-325, 1983 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 466, at *4. Therefore, the amended return does not affect Pinckney’s correct tax liability.
III. Pinckney is liable for penalties under section 6663(a) for 2008 and 2009.
Section 6663(a) provides that “[i]f any part of any underpayment of tax required to be shown on a return is due to fraud, there shall be
34 Nor is there any evidence that Pinckney sought an extension of the March 15, 2009, deadline under Rev. Proc. 2004-48. Even had he filed for such an extension in 2012 (i.e., at the same time the IRS concedes he filed the Form 2553), this would have been long after September 15, 2010, the deadline for filing for such an extension, i.e., six months from March 15, 2010, the tax year 2009 Form 1120–S filing due date, and 18 months from March 15, 2009, the S corporation election deadline. See §§ 7503, 1362(b)(1); Zaimes, T.C. Memo. 2023-121, at *6.
163
[*163] added to the tax an amount equal to 75 percent of the portion of the underpayment which is attributable to fraud.” Section 7454(a) provides that “[i]n any proceeding involving the issue whether the petitioner has been guilty of fraud with intent to evade tax, the burden of proof in respect of such issue shall be upon the Secretary.” Under Rule 142(b), the burden of proof imposed by section 7454(a) is satisfied only by evidence that is clear and convincing: “In any case involving the issue of fraud with intent to evade tax, the burden of proof in respect of that issue is on the respondent, and that burden of proof is to be carried by clear and convincing evidence.” In order to discharge the burden imposed by section 7454(a) as to liability under section 6663(a), the IRS must prove two elements by clear and convincing evidence: (1) that there exists an underpayment for the tax year at issue, and (2) that “some portion of the underpayment is due to fraud.” Petzoldt v. Commissioner, 92 T.C. 661, 699 (1989); see also Hebrank v. Commissioner, 81 T.C. 640, 642 (1983).
As to the first element, the IRS need only prove the existence of an underpayment; it need not prove the amount of the underpayment. Zack v. Commissioner, 692 F.2d 28, 29 (6th Cir. 1982), aff’g T.C. Memo. 1981-700; DiLeo, 96 T.C. at 873; Shaw v. Commissioner, 27 T.C. 561, 570 (1956), aff’d, 252 F.2d 681 (6th Cir. 1958).
As to the second element, because direct evidence of fraudulent intent is seldom available, the IRS may prove fraud by circumstantial evidence and reasonable inferences drawn from the facts. Spies v. United States, 317 U.S. 492, 499–500 (1943); Niedringhaus v. Commissioner, 99 T.C. 202, 210 (1992). The taxpayer’s entire course of conduct may be indicative of fraudulent intent. Niedringhaus, 99 T.C. at 210; Stone v. Commissioner, 56 T.C. 213, 223–24 (1971); Otsuki v. Commissioner, 53 T.C. 96, 105–06 (1969).
If the IRS proves both elements, the total amount of the underpayment for the year is then determined under the ordinary burden-of-proof rules, including the rule that the taxpayer has the burden of persuasion as to determinations in the notice of deficiency. Zack v. Commissioner, 692 F.2d at 29.
Once the total amount of the underpayment for the year has thus been determined, and if the IRS has proven by clear and convincing evidence the existence of a portion of the underpayment and that this portion of the underpayment is due to fraud, it remains to be determined how much of the rest of the underpayment is due to the fraud. This
164
[*164] standard for making this determination is set forth in section 6663(b). Section 6663(b) provides that if the IRS “establishes that any portion of an underpayment is attributable to fraud, the entire underpayment shall be treated as attributable to fraud, except with respect to any portion of the underpayment which the taxpayer establishes (by a preponderance of the evidence) is not attributable to fraud.” See also Michael I. Saltzman & Leslie Book, IRS Practice and Procedure ¶ 7B.02[1] (2025), Westlaw IRSPRAC (“Therefore, at least theoretically, if the Service proves that a taxpayer failed to report $100 of interest on a bank account held in an assumed name, every other adjustment is treated as due to fraud, unless the taxpayer proves otherwise by a preponderance of the evidence.”).
Under section 6751(b), the section 6663(a) fraud penalty must be approved in writing by the immediate supervisor of the IRS employee who initially determined to impose the penalty. See § 7491(c); Clay v. Commissioner, 152 T.C. 223, 248–50 (2019), aff’d, 990 F.3d 1296 (11th Cir. 2021); Graev v. Commissioner, 149 T.C. 485, 493 (2017), supplementing and overruling in part 147 T.C. 460 (2016). The written approval of the initial penalty determination must be obtained before the proposed penalty is first formally communicated to the taxpayer in writing. Frost v. Commissioner, 154 T.C. 23, 32–35 (2020); Belair Woods, LLC v. Commissioner, 154 T.C. 1, 15 (2020); Clay, 152 T.C. at 248–50; see also Graev, 149 T.C. at 493. In this case, the IRS satisfied the section 6751(b) requirements for imposing the section 6663 fraud penalties. The IRS’s first formal communication of the section 6663 penalties to Pinckney was the 2018 30-day letter. This letter, dated May 11, 2018, reflected an initial determination, made by Myers, to assert the fraud penalties. The letter was signed by Norris, Myers’s immediate supervisor, reflecting that she had approved the determination to assert the fraud penalties before the determination was communicated to Pinckney. Thus, the fraud penalties against Pinckney for 2008 and 2009 were supervisor approved under section 6751(b).35
We now consider whether Pinckney is liable for the fraud penalties. This determination is made on a year-by-year basis. See Vannaman v. Commissioner, 54 T.C. 1011, 1017 (1970); Temple v.
35 Our conclusion that the initial determination of the penalties was supervisor approved is supported by the preponderance of the evidence. A fortiori, the IRS has met its burden of production of coming forward with evidence of proper penalty approval as part of his initial burden of production under section 7491(c). Frost, 154 T.C. at 34.
165
[*165] Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2000-337, 2000 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 395, at *36 (citing Drieborg v. Commissioner, 225 F.2d 216, 219–20 (6th Cir. 1955)), aff’d, 62 F. App’x 605 (6th Cir. 2003).
A. Pinckney is liable for the section 6663(a) fraud penalty for tax year 2008.
1. The IRS has proven by clear and convincing evidence that Daitech’s cost of goods sold was zero rather than $23,231.
On his 2008 return Pinckney reported a tax liability of $5,903. The Notice of Deficiency made eight noncomputational adjustments to items reported on Pinckney’s 2008 return and, as a result of these adjustments, the IRS determined that his tax liability for the year was $77,484, for a deficiency of $71,581 (equal to $77,484 – $5,903). We did not fully sustain the adjustment in the Notice of Deficiency to Daitech’s gross receipts, see supra Part I.C, and therefore the deficiency we redetermine is less than the deficiency determined by the Notice of Deficiency. The deficiency we redetermine is the result of the following eight noncomputational errors Pinckney made on his return: (1) his failure to report $3,352 of interest income; (2) his failure to report $453 of gross receipts of Pinckney Business Consulting; (3) his report that Daitech had gross receipts of $200,500, rather than $376,712 as determined by the Court; (4) his erroneous claim of $4,776 of businessexpense deductions related to Pinckney Business Consulting; (5) his claim that Daitech had cost of goods sold of $23,231 instead of zero; (6) his claim of $141,883 of business-expense deductions related to Daitech, instead of $132,444 as determined by the Notice of Deficiency; (7) his claim of a home-mortgage interest deduction of $69,981, rather than $39,667 as determined by the Notice of Deficiency; and (8) his claim of charitable-contribution deductions of $5,350, rather than $4,840 as determined by the Notice of Deficiency.
Each of the eight errors resulting in a deficiency for 2008 also potentially produces an underpayment for 2008 equal to the deficiency. This is so because, statutorily, a deficiency is defined generally the same as an underpayment. See §§ 6664(a) (defining “underpayment” generally as “the amount by which any tax imposed . . . exceeds . . . the amount shown as the tax by the taxpayer on his return”), 6211(a) (defining “deficiency” generally as “the amount by which the tax imposed . . . exceeds . . . the amount shown as the tax by the taxpayer upon his return”).
166
[*166] However, burden-of-proof rules for determining a deficiency are different from burden-of-proof rules for determining an underpayment in a fraud case. As explained above, our redetermination of a deficiency for 2008 is the result of Pinckney’s bearing the burden of persuasion. The IRS’s burden of proving the existence of an underpayment under section 7454(a) cannot be satisfied merely by the taxpayer’s failure to satisfy the burden of persuasion. See Parks v. Commissioner, 94 T.C. 654, 660–61 (1990); Otsuki, 53 T.C. at 106. Only if the IRS establishes one or more of the eight errors described above by clear and convincing evidence has it established the existence of an underpayment by clear and convincing evidence.
In our view, the IRS has established by clear and convincing evidence that Daitech had no cost-of-goods sold for 2008. Daitech sold services, not goods. Furthermore, Pinckney submitted a Form 1120–S for Daitech in 2012 that did not report a cost-of-goods-sold amount for Daitech. This is an admission that the cost-of-goods-sold amount should not have been claimed on Pinckney’s individual return. See Lare v. Commissioner, 62 T.C. 739, 750 (1974) (“Statements made in a tax return . . . may be treated as admissions.”), aff’d, 521 F.2d 1399 (3d Cir. 1975) (unpublished table decision). In conclusion, the IRS has established by clear and convincing evidence that there existed an underpayment for 2008 resulting from the false claim of cost of goods sold for Daitech.
2. The IRS has proven by clear and convincing evidence that Pinckney’s claim that Daitech’s cost of goods sold was $23,231 was fraudulent.
We also conclude that the IRS has established that a portion of the underpayment for 2008 (the portion resulting from the report of the cost-of-goods-sold amount) was due to fraud. We are persuaded that this is the case by the following:
Daitech did not sell goods.
Pinckney conceded the cost of goods sold should not have been
reported on his return.
167
[*167]
One of Pinckney’s techniques for defrauding the IRS was to
falsely report for cost of goods sold on the Schedule Cs of Daitech customers.36
Pinckney pleaded guilty in 2015 to conspiracy to submit false
income tax returns to the IRS in violation of 18 U.S.C. § 371, and he was judged guilty of this violation by a U.S. district court. See Mobley v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 1993-60, 1993 WL 42836, at *5 (relying on conviction under 18 U.S.C. § 371 as indicator of fraud), aff’d, 33 F.3d 1382 (11th Cir. 1994) (unpublished table opinion).37
During the trial of Samuels in 2016, Pinckney admitted that
he lied to the IRS “throughout” the 2008 return and that by filing the return he “evaded taxes at least to the sum of $63,601.”
Pinckney argues that we erred by admitting certain documents from his criminal proceedings. We quote the argument from Pinckney’s Opening Brief:
The Respondent was allowed by the court to make the Petitioner’s complaint a fraud case by allowing testimony from Petitioner’s criminal case involving Restitution to include grand jury records and witness testimony. Court failed to remember that the Petitioner Sued [sic] the
36 Pinckney contends that it is improper for us to conclude that Daitech’s client tax returns were false unless the IRS examines these returns. We disagree. The trial record includes testimony by several Daitech clients and an employee that Daitech filed false returns for its clients. We find this testimony credible and therefore base properly our conclusion on this testimony. No legal authority exists that limits this Court’s ability to make factual determinations sans IRS examinations.
37 The IRS does not argue that Pinckney’s conviction under 18 U.S.C. § 371 collaterally estops him from denying that there were underpayments due to fraud under I.R.C. § 6663(a), as it might have argued had Pinckney been convicted under I.R.C. § 7201. See Williams v. Commissioner, 498 F. App’x 284, 288–89 (4th Cir. 2012) (first citing Moore v. United States, 360 F.2d 353, 355 (4th Cir. 1965), mandate recalled by 17 A.F.T.R.2d (RIA) 374 (4th Cir. 1966); then citing DiLeo, 96 T.C. at 885–86; and then citing United States v. Wight, 839 F.2d 193, 196 (4th Cir. 1987)) (“A taxpayer is collaterally estopped from denying civil tax fraud when convicted for criminal tax evasion under 26 U.S.C. § 7201 for the same taxable year.” (emphasis added)), aff’g T.C. Memo. 2011-89; cf. Wright v. Commissioner, 84 T.C. 636 (1985) (holding that a conviction under I.R.C. § 7206(1) does not collaterally estop a taxpayer from denying under I.R.C. § 6653(b) that part of his or her underpayment was due to fraud).
168
[*168]
Respondent and not the other way around. The Court allowed the Petitioner to be prosecuted all over again.
. . . .
A Trial de Novo is a new trial or a criminal retrial in which the entire case is presented as if there had been no previous trial. In a trial de novo, new evidence and witness testimony can be presented “without deference to the initial judgment”- [sic] meaning that the outcome of the previous trial is not considered. Respondent referred the Petitioner’s case as a Trial De novo Deficiency case. Yet, the Respondent was allowed by the court to use testimony from the Petitioner’s criminal trial, grand jury records, and other trial documents to present their case on a deficiency matter.
Pet.’s Opening Brief at 4, 14, Doc. No. 80 (citations omitted). Although there are a number of documents that might seemingly be implicated by Pinckney’s argument (including, for example, Exhibit 9-J, the criminal judgment against Pinckney; Exhibit 10-J, the superseding indictment by the grand jury against Pinckney; Exhibits 11-J and 117-R, excerpts of Pinckney’s testimony in United States v. Samuels, No. 3:14-CR-606- JMC; and Exhibit 125-R, Pinckney’s plea agreement), the only document Pinckney’s brief identifies with specificity is the superseding grand jury indictment. See Pet.’s Opening Brief at 14 ¶ 17, Doc. No. 80. At trial, Pinckney failed to object to the admissibility of this document. Pinckney has thus failed to preserve his objection to its admissibility. We therefore find that Pinckney’s untimely and unspecific argument on brief does not merit consideration by the Court.
3. Pinckney has failed to prove by a preponderance of the evidence that his underpayment for 2008 is less than the deficiency we redetermine for 2008.
Because the IRS has proven by clear and convincing evidence that a portion of the underpayment for 2008 is due to fraud, it is up to Pinckney to persuade us that the IRS’s determination of the amount of the total underpayment for 2008 is incorrect. See Zack v. Commissioner, 692 F.2d at 29. This Pinckney has failed to do, with the exception of the $5,531 amount that the gross receipts of Daitech determined by the Notice of Deficiency ($382,243) exceed the gross receipts we have determined ($376,712). Therefore, the amount of the underpayment is
169
[*169] not the amount determined in the Notice of Deficiency but will be redetermined by the Court under Rule 155. Because an underpayment is ordinarily the same as a deficiency, the amount of the underpayment may well be the same amount as the deficiency we redetermine under Rule 155.
4. Pinckney failed to prove by a preponderance of the evidence that any portion of the underpayment for 2008 is not attributable to fraud.
Under section 6663, the entire amount of the underpayment for 2008 is due to fraud except for any portion of the underpayment that Pinckney has established by a preponderance of the evidence is not attributable to fraud. § 6663(b) (“If the Secretary establishes that any portion of an underpayment is attributable to fraud, the entire underpayment shall be treated as attributable to fraud, except with respect to any portion of the underpayment which the taxpayer establishes (by a preponderance of the evidence) is not attributable to fraud.”). Pinckney has failed to establish by a preponderance of the evidence that any portion of the underpayment for 2008 is not attributable to fraud. Therefore, the entire amount of the underpayment for 2008 is due to fraud.
B. Pinckney is liable for the section 6663(a) fraud penalty for tax year 2009.
1. The IRS has proven by clear and convincing evidence that Daitech’s cost of goods sold was zero rather than $30,667.
On his 2009 return, Pinckney reported a tax liability of $10,406. The Notice of Deficiency made eight noncomputational adjustments to the items reported on Pinckney’s 2009 return and, as a result of these adjustments, the IRS determined that his tax liability for the year was $85,754, for a deficiency of $75,638 (equal to $85,754 − $10,406 + $290). We did not fully sustain the adjustment in the Notice of Deficiency to Daitech’s business-expense deductions, see supra Part II.F, and therefore the deficiency we redetermine is less than the deficiency determined by the Notice of Deficiency. The deficiency we redetermine is the result of the following eight noncomputational errors Pinckney made on his return: (1) his failure to report $20 of other income; (2) his failure to report $445 of capital-gain income; (3) his failure to report $292 of dividend income; (4) his report that Daitech had gross receipts
170
[*170] of $143,080, rather than $340,765; (5) his claim that Daitech’s cost of goods sold was $30,667, rather than zero; (6) his claim that Daitech’s business-expense deductions were $60,202, rather than the $66,963 determined by the Court; (7) his claim of a $5,000 health-care deduction; and (8) his claim of a charitable-contribution deduction of $4,350, rather than $3,850. Each of the eight errors resulting in a deficiency for 2009 also potentially produce an underpayment for 2009 equal to the deficiency. As with 2008, see supra Part III.A.1, the IRS has established by clear and convincing evidence that Daitech’s cost of goods sold for 2009 is zero rather than the amount Pinckney reported. Daitech sold services, not goods. Furthermore, Pinckney submitted a Form 1120–S for Daitech in 2012 that did not claim a cost-of-goods-sold amount for Daitech, thereby admitting no such reduction is warranted. In conclusion, the IRS has established by clear and convincing evidence that there existed an underpayment for 2009 resulting from the false claim of cost of goods sold for Daitech.
2. The IRS has proven by clear and convincing evidence that Pinckney’s claim that Daitech’s cost of goods sold was $30,667 was due to fraud.
We find that the cost-of-goods-sold amount Pinckney claimed for Daitech for 2009 is fraudulent. Our reasons are similar to our reasons for finding that the cost-of-goods-sold amount reported on the 2008 return is fraudulent: Daitech did not sell goods; Pinckney conceded on the amended return that the reduction for cost of goods sold should not have been claimed on his original return; Pinckney made false claims of cost of goods sold on the returns of Daitech customers; Pinckney pleaded guilty and a criminal judgment was entered against him; and Pinckney, in his testimony during the Samuels trial, testified that he “lied” on his 2009 return.
3. Pinckney failed to prove by a preponderance of the evidence that his underpayment for 2009 is less than the deficiency we determine for 2009.
Because the IRS has proven by clear and convincing evidence that a portion of the total underpayment for 2009 is due to fraud, it is up to Pinckney to persuade us that the IRS’s determination of the amount of the underpayment—$75,638—is incorrect. See Zack v. Commissioner, 692 F.2d at 29. This Pinckney has failed to do, with the exception of the $3,764 amount that the business-expense deductions of Daitech we have determined ($66,963) exceed the business-expense deductions of Daitech
171
[*171] determined by the Notice of Deficiency ($63,199). Therefore, the amount of the underpayment is not the amount determined in the Notice of Deficiency but will be determined by the Court under Rule 155. Because an underpayment is ordinarily the same as a deficiency, the amount of the underpayment may well be the same amount of the deficiency we redetermine under Rule 155.
4. Pinckney failed to prove by a preponderance of the evidence that any portion of his underpayment for 2009 is not attributable to fraud.
Under section 6663(b), the entire amount of the underpayment for 2009 is due to fraud except with respect to any portion of the underpayment that Pinckney has established by a preponderance of the evidence is not attributable to fraud. Pinckney has failed to establish by a preponderance of the evidence that any portion of the underpayment for 2009 is not attributable to fraud. Therefore, the entire amount of the underpayment for 2009 is due to fraud.
IV. The periods of limitation for assessment as to tax years 2008 and 2009 did not expire before the IRS mailed the Notice of Deficiency.
Under section 6501(a), the IRS generally must assess tax within three years after the filing date of the return. A return filed before the due date for filing the return (determined without regard to the extension of time for filing) is deemed to be filed on the due date. § 6501(b); Treas. Reg. § 301.6501(a)-1(a). The issuance of a notice of deficiency suspends the running of the period for assessment. § 6503(a)(1). But if the period for assessment has already run by the time the IRS mails the notice of deficiency, the IRS is barred from assessing the deficiency. Estate of Robinson v. Commissioner, 101 T.C. 499, 516 (1993); Blount v. Commissioner, 86 T.C. 383, 387 (1986).
There are three exceptions (of interest here) to the general rule of section 6501(a) that the period for assessing tax is three years. First, under section 6501(c)(4), the taxpayer and the IRS may, before the expiration of the period for assessment, agree to extend the period for assessment. Second, under section 6501(e)(1), the period for assessment is within six years after the filing date of the return if the amount of gross income omitted from the return exceeds 25% of the gross income reported on the return. See also § 6501(b)(1). Third, under section 6501(c)(1), assessment may occur at any time “[i]n the case of a false or fraudulent return with the intent to evade tax.”
172
[*172] On May 12, 2009, Pinckney filed his 2008 return, thus meeting the extended filing deadline of October 15, 2009. On January 20, 2012, Pinckney consented to extend the period for assessing 2008 tax to December 31, 2013. An entry in the IRS records (dated November 18, 2014) stated that the IRS can assess tax for 2008 until May 12, 2015, apparently a reference to the six-year period for assessment.
Meanwhile, on April 6, 2010, Pinckney filed his 2009 return, thus meeting the extended filing deadline of October 15, 2010. However, IRS records state that the return was not received until September 24, 2010. An entry in the IRS records (dated March 4, 2015) stated that the IRS can assess tax for 2009 until September 24, 2013, apparently a reference to the three-year period for assessment but calculated with reference to the date the IRS had recorded that it received the return.
On February 6, 2019, the IRS mailed Pinckney the Notice of Deficiency, which related to the 2008 and 2009 tax years.
Pinckney claims that the assessment periods for 2008 and 2009 have run. In his view, the period for assessing tax for both tax years is the six-year period of section 6501(e)(1). If that view is correct, then the period for assessing tax for tax year 2008 expired on October 15, 2015, and the period for assessing tax for tax year 2009 expired on October 15, 2016. See § 6501(b)(1). Pinckney also contends that the assessment period for 2008 has expired because the IRS failed to issue the Notice of Deficiency by the December 31, 2013, date to which he consented to extend the period for assessment. He further contends that extending the assessment date to December 31, 2013, was improper under section 6501(c)(4). He also contends that the assessment period for 2009 has expired because no agreement to extend the period was made under section 6501(c)(4).
The IRS’s response is simple: The periods for assessing tax for tax years 2008 and 2009 are still open under section 6501(c)(1) because Pinckney’s returns for those years were false and fraudulent with intent to evade tax.
As we have found, see supra Part III, Pinckney’s 2008 and 2009 returns were false and fraudulent with the intent to evade tax. Therefore, pursuant to section 6501(c)(1), assessment of tax and the fraud penalties for these years is not time barred by section 6501(a). See Rhone-Poulenc Surfactants & Specialties, L.P. v. Commissioner, 114 T.C. 533, 548 (2000); Otsuki, 53 T.C. at 114. We need not consider the
173
[*173] operation of other exceptions to section 6501(a), such as section 6501(c)(4) and (e)(1).38
V. In this deficiency proceeding, this Court does not have jurisdiction to determine whether the restitution-based assessments violated section 6201(a)(4)(B).
Pinckney makes the following argument in his Opening Brief:
The Respondent violated the Petitioner’s Due process of law by filing a tax Lien on his property for the tax years 2008 and 2009 while the case is still in appeal in violation of U.S. Code 6201(a)(4) [sic](B) which states an assessment of an amount of restitution under an order described in subparagraph (A) shall not be made before all appeals of such order are concluded and the right to make all such appeals has expired.
Recall the following background regarding the restitution assessment. On December 1, 2016, the U.S. district court entered judgment requiring Pinckney to pay restitution of $334,738.02. On October 15, 2019, the IRS Criminal Investigation Division approved assessment of the $334,738.02, whereby $68,982.38 would be assessed for tax year 2008 ($21,284 for Pinckney’s clients and $47,698.38 for Pinckney’s own liability) and $152,015.88 would be assessed for tax year 2009 ($44,464 for Pinckney’s clients and $107,551.88 for Pinckney’s own liability). On January 15, 2020, the IRS assessed a $66,032.78 restitution liability against Pinckney for tax year 2008. On November 23, 2020, the IRS assessed a $145,517.88 restitution liability against Pinckney for tax year 2009. On June 10, 2021, the IRS issued a notice to Pinckney that it
38 Pinckney also claims that collection of the deficiencies is barred by section 6502(a):
IRC Sec. 6502, IRS has 10 years to collect a liability from date of assessment. The collection statute expiration ends the government’s right to pursue collection of a liability. Each tax assessment has a Collection Statute Expiration Date (CSED). Internal Revenue Code section 6502 provides that the length of the period for collection after assessment of a tax liability is 10 years. Time has expired to collect.
Section 6502(a) provides that a tax that has been assessed must be collected within ten years after the date of assessment. The IRS has not yet assessed the deficiencies and fraud penalties for 2008 and 2009. Indeed, it is prevented from doing so by section 6212(a), which provides that no assessment of a deficiency shall be made until a notice of deficiency is mailed to a taxpayer nor, if a petition has been filed with the Tax Court, until the decision of the Tax Court has become final.
174
[*174] had filed a notice of federal tax lien with respect to the assessed $66,032.78 restitution liability for 2008. On June 29, 2021, the IRS filed a notice of federal tax lien with respect to the assessed $145,517.88 restitution liability for 2009. Respondent mailed Pinckney the Notice of Deficiency on February 6, 2019, determining deficiencies of $71,581 for 2008 and $75,638 for 2009. The deficiencies have not been assessed.
Section 6201(a)(4)(A) provides that the IRS “shall assess and collect the amount of restitution under an order pursuant to [18 U.S.C. § 3556] for failure to pay any tax imposed under [the Internal Revenue Code of 1986]in the same manner as if such amount were such tax.” 18 U.S.C § 3556 requires district courts to order restitution in accordance with 18 U.S.C. § 3663A, which in turn requires district courts to order restitution to victims of the crime. Section 6201(a)(4)(B) provides that an assessment of an amount under section 6201(a)(4)(A) “shall not be made before all appeals of such order [i.e., an order pursuant to 18 U.S.C. § 3556] are concluded and the right to make all such appeals has expired.” Pinckney contends that section 6201(a)(4)(B) prevents the IRS from making an assessment of the U.S. district court’s restitution judgment under the mistaken view that the “appeal[] of such order” is the instant case.
In deficiency proceedings such as this one, the Tax Court has jurisdiction under section 6213(a) to enjoin assessments of deficiencies made before the decision of the Tax Court is final. A deficiency is generally the unreported tax liability. § 6211(a). Although restitution is based upon an estimate of tax liability, it is not same as the civil tax liability. Klein v. Commissioner, 149 T.C. 341, 359, 361 (2017); Rozin v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2017-52, at *5. It follows that the IRS’s assessment of restitution is not a violation of the section 6213(a) prohibition on assessment of deficiencies. The Court in this instant case is therefore without power to enjoin the restitution assessments made with respect to Pinckney on the ground that the assessments were made before the Tax Court had made a final decision. Additionally, Pinckney’s grievance against the restitution assessments is grounded on section 6201(a)(4)(B), which bars premature assessments of district court restitution orders, and nothing in section 6213(a) gives us the power to remedy a section 6201(a)(4)(B) violation.39
39 By contrast, if Pinckney had requested a collection-due-process hearing in response to the June 10, 2021, notice of filing of federal tax lien as to the 2008 tax year, he could have made his section 6201(a)(4)(B) argument with the IRS Office of Appeals
175
[*175] Even if we had the power to entertain Pinckney’s theory that the assessments of restitution by the IRS violated section 6201(a)(4)(B), Pinckney has failed to prove the factual predicates of his theory. The judgment in Pinckney’s criminal case was entered against him on December 1, 2016. Nothing in the record in this case shows that Pinckney filed an appeal of that judgment. Nor could he have. Pursuant to his plea agreement, Pinckney waived the right to contest his conviction or the sentence via any direct or collateral appeal. Even if Pinckney could have appealed, the opportunity for a direct appeal expired on December 15, 2016, see Fed. R. App. P. 4(b)(1)(A)(i), and Pinckney never had the right to collaterally attack the restitution order via a habeas challenge, see, e.g., Kaminski v. United States, 339 F.3d 84, 87, 89 (2d Cir. 2003) (“Habeas lies to allow attacks on wrongful custodies [only].” Id. at 89.). The assessments Pinckney complains of took place on January 15, 2020 (for the 2008 tax year), and November 23, 2020 (for the 2009 tax year). Thus, Pinckney has failed to prove that the restitution-based assessments were made before all appeals of the judgment were concluded and the right to make such appeals had expired.
VI. Pinckney’s argument that Myers misrepresented the nature of the examination, and that this alleged misrepresentation violated his Fourth Amendment rights, lacks merit.
Pinckney argues that Myers violated his Fourth Amendment rights by failing to tell Pinckney that he was conducting a criminal investigation. In relevant detail, Pinckney writes in his brief that
Before or on August 6, 2010, the IRS was developing a criminal Fraud case against Mr. Pinckney under the guise of a civil investigation [sic]which is a direct violation of IRM section 4565.21 of their manual in an [sic] effect violated his Fourth and Fifth Amendment (Due process Clause) rights. On September 30, 2010 [sic] Petitioner was
in a collection-due-process hearing and, if the determination of that Office went against him, he could have made the argument before the Tax Court in an appeal from that determination. See §§ 6320(a), (b)(1), (c), 6330(b)(1), (c)(2); Carpenter v. Commissioner, 152 T.C. 202, 210–11 (2009) (“Significantly, Congress did allow the taxpayer to retain the ability to challenge the Secretary’s method of collecting an RBA [restitution-based assessment].”), aff’d, 788 F. App’x 187 (4th Cir. 2019); Ervin v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2021-75, at *9 (finding, in appeal from collection-due-process determination by IRS Office of Appeals, that the IRS properly made assessment of restitution after the restitution order became final).
176
[*176]
called in for an examination of his 2008 Tax Returns by Revenue Agent Carlton Myers (Exs.97-P,29-J) (Gov. Transcript 522 (12-25), 555 (20-25), 556 (1-25), 557 (1-6)). Mr. Myers was acting as a prosecutor during the audit rather than an auditor.
. . . .
Revenue Agent Carlton Myers had been developing a criminal fraud case against Petitioner under the guise of a civil investigation violating IRM section 4565.21 [sic] Mr. Myers had been receiving assistance by CID Special Agent Robert Keyes rather than the assigned Fraud Technical Advisor (FTA) [sic] which is a direct violation of Petitioner’s Fourth and Fifth Amendment due process rights (Ex. 95- J). . . . Courts of Appeal [sic] have consistently held that an IRS criminal investigation conducted under the guise of a civil audit violates a defendant’s Fourth and Fifth Amendment rights. See, e.g., U.S. v. McKee, 192 F.3d 535, 541-42 n.5 (6th Cir. 1999); U.S. v. Powell, 835 F.2d 1095, 1100 (5th Cir. 1988); U.S. v. Tweel, 550 F.2d 297, 300 (5th Cir. 1977).
. . . .
On April 5, 2012 [sic] another Form 4549 was issued by Mr. Myers (Ex. 20-P). The Petitioner was never placed on notice by Revenue Agent Carlton Myers that the audit will be for civil [sic] not criminal purposes (Gov. Transcript 945(17- 20). Totally unaware of the true purpose[sic] which evidence was gathered [sic] which is violative of his Fourth Amendment Right.
. . . .
On April 05, 2012, Mr. Myers generated a civil audit Tax Assessment Report Form 4549 while still in continuous conversation with New CID Special Agent Robert Keyes (who was 6 months on the job and very inexperienced as well with the process of handling fraud cases). While a civil audit is being conducted, Mr. Keyes is conversing directly with Mr. Myers and not a [sic] FTA. Mr. Keyes was advised by his supervisor to end conversations [sic] immediately (Ex. 95-J). As admitted in court by Respondent’s Attorney
177
[*177]
[sic] Mr. Prosky [sic] Petitioner’s civil audit was still ongoing in April 2012 (Gov. Transcript 964(4-8). Civil Auditor Carlton Myers conducted a civil audit Tax assessment [sic] was [sic] in April 2012 while Petitioner was arrested by Criminal Investigations Division in May 2012 (Exs. 19-P,20-J [sic]).
We begin by discussing the caselaw on which Pinckney relies for his argument. In Tweel, 550 F.2d 297, the first case he cites, the taxpayer’s representative asked the IRS examiner whether the examiner was working with a “special agent” (i.e., an IRS criminal investigator). Id. at 298. The examiner replied that no special agent was “involved.” Id. The examiner failed to reveal that the examination had been opened at the request of government criminal prosecutors. Id. Assuming that the examination was purely civil, the taxpayer’s representative gave the examiner information that the government later attempted to use in a criminal prosecution. Id. The U.S. Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit held that the examiner deceived the taxpayer by failing to “apprise [him] of the obvious criminal nature of this investigation,” and consequently, his Fourth Amendment right against unreasonable searches and seizures had been violated; and that the evidence turned over to the examiner should be suppressed in the criminal prosecution. Id. at 299– 300.40
In McKee, 192 F.3d 535, also cited by Pinckney, the U.S. Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit opined that a taxpayer’s constitutional rights are violated if the IRS violates old IRM 4565.21(1) (the predecessor to 25.1.3.3 of current IRM) which stated in part: “Once there is a firm indication of criminal fraud all examination activity must be suspended.”41
40 Tweel does not mention old IRM 4565.21. The alleged misconduct occurred in 1969, Tweel, 550 F.2d at 298–99, which was old IRM 4565.21 was adopted.
41 The portion of old IRM 4565.21(1) quoted by McKee, 192 F.2d 535 at 541–42, was:
If, during an examination, an examiner [i.e., revenue agent] uncovers a potentially fraudulent situation caused by the taxpayer and or the preparer, the examiner shall discuss the case at the earliest possible convenience with his/her group manager. . . . Once there is a firm indication of criminal fraud all examination activity shall be suspended.
The relevant portion of current IRM 25.1.3.3 states:
178
[*178] The final legal authority Pinckney cites is Powell, 835 F.2d 1095, in which the Fifth Circuit considered a taxpayer’s allegations that an IRS examiner had violated the standard set forth in Tweel and had violated old IRM 4565.21(1). Addressing the Tweel standard, Powell, 835 F.2d at 1099, stated that in fact the IRS had not been conducting a criminal investigation while the examiner was involved in the case. Therefore, Powell held there was no “deceit, trickery or misrepresentation [by] the Internal Revenue agent.” Id. at 1098 (quoting Tweel, 550 F.2d at 299). Powell held that the revenue agent did not violate old IRM 4565.21 (because, Powell held, there was sufficient evidence the IRS examiner did not have a firm indication there was fraud), and that therefore it was unnecessary to decide whether the Internal Revenue Manual provision created a constitutionally protected right. Powell, 835 F.2d at 1100–01.
Assuming––without deciding––that it is appropriate to employ the approach taken in Powell, we would find no constitutional violation, because (1) the examiner in this case (Myers) did not misrepresent the nature of the examination as in Tweel and (2) the record does not show that the examiner had a firm indication of fraud at any time before the civil examination was suspended, and thus, the examiner did not violate old IRM 4565.21(1).42 We elaborate on these two conclusions as we review the relevant facts:
Recall that on August 6, 2010, the IRS assigned Myers to examine
Pinckney’s 2008 return, which had been selected randomly.
On September 21, 2010, Myers mailed Pinckney an initial contact
letter for 2008 regarding the civil examination.
If . . . it is determined that a potential fraud case has firm indications of fraud/willfulness and meets criminal criteria, the compliance employee will suspend the examination/collection activity without disclosing to the taxpayer or representative the reason for the suspension. When the taxpayer asks if a fraud referral is being considered or whether CI [(Criminal Investigation)] is involved, the examiner or revenue officer must not give a false or deceitful response.
IRM 25.1.3.3(3) (Nov. 17, 2023). Because old 4565.21(1) is similar to current IRM 25.1.3.3, we hereinafter analyze the wording of old IRM 4565.21(1).
42 The Fourth Circuit has held that a violation of old IRM 4565.21 is not conclusive evidence of a Fourth Amendment violation but is potentially relevant to a showing of a Fourth Amendment violation. Groder v. United States, 816 F.2d 139, 142 (4th Cir. 1987). Because Myers did not violate old IRM 4565.21, we need not evaluate the legal significance of such a violation.
179
[*179]
On September 30, 2010, Myers interviewed Pinckney. The record
does not reveal much about what was discussed at this interview. Pinckney contends on brief that Myers testified at trial that he did not advise Pinckney at this interview that the examination was civil. Pinckney had asked Myers at trial the following question: “And once Mr. Pinckney came in for the examination, did you inform Mr. Pinckney that the audit would be used for civil, not criminal purposes?” Myers answered, “No.” Although this testimony is not entirely clear, we agree with Pinckney that Myers did not advise Pinckney at the September 30, 2010, interview that the examination was civil. This does not suggest that Myers misrepresented the nature of the examination. The examination was civil, not criminal, and thus failing to advise Pinckney that the examination was civil did not mislead Pinckney.
On February 11, 2011, Myers expanded the examination to tax
year 2009.
On January 19, 2012, Myers issued Pinckney a 30-day letter
regarding 2008 and 2009. Although Pinckney alleges that Myers was working with Key at this point, the record does not support this allegation. Myers credibly testified that he was not working with the Criminal Investigation Division during the examination.
On April 11, 2012, Key told Myers to suspend the examination.
Key shortly followed up with an email to Myers to direct that Myers should have no further contact with Pinckney. Although Pinckney contends that this email shows that Myers had been receiving assistance from Key all along, the email evidences only that Key and Myers had the conversation on April 11, 2012. Myers testified convincingly that this was his only conversation with Key.
By April 11, 2012, the civil examination had been suspended.
Pinckney contends that he was arrested sometime in May 2012.
However, the date of his arrest is not in the record. Therefore, we draw no conclusions about the date of the arrest.
On May 7, 2012, Myers’ supervisor emailed Key to ask the status
of the criminal case. The same day, Key emailed Myers’
180
[*180]
supervisor, instructing her to continue holding the civil case in suspense.
On August 20, 2014, a grand jury indicted Pinckney.
As explained in our review of the events above, when Myers was conducting the examination regarding Pinckney from August 6, 2010, to April 11, 2012, Myers was not involved in or aware of any criminal investigation of Pinckney. Therefore, Myers did not “misrepresent” the civil nature of the examination. In short, the examination was indeed civil. See Powell, 835 F.2d at 1099.
We find that at no point during the pre-suspension portion of the examination (i.e., August 6, 2010, to April 11, 2012) did Myers discover a “firm indication of criminal fraud” within the meaning of old IRM 4565.21(1). The trial record does not show what evidence of fraud was found in documents sent to him by Pinckney, or in statements made to him by Pinckney, in the pre-suspension portion of the examination. By May 11, 2018, when he wrote an internal memo, Myers had concluded that the 2008 and 2009 returns were fraudulent, but this was after the examination had been resumed, and after Pinckney had been criminally investigated and pleaded guilty. Thus, this internal memo does not show that Myers had discovered a “firm indication of fraud” during the presuspension portion of the examination from August 6, 2010, to April 11, 2012.
We conclude that even under the Powell analysis urged by Pinckney, Myers did not violate Pinckney’s constitutional rights. It is therefore unnecessary for us to determine what remedy would be appropriate had Myers violated Pinckney’s constitutional rights. See Weiss v. Commissioner, 919 F.2d 115, 118–19 (9th Cir. 1990), aff’g T.C. Memo. 1988-586; Vallone v. Commissioner, 88 T.C. 794, 813 (1987); Scharringhausen v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2012-350, at *29–30.
VII. The Court did not commit any prejudicial errors during the trial.
A. The Court did not err in having the IRS present its case first.
Pinckney contends that the Court erred in having the IRS present its case first. The trial judge has discretion over the order of proof. Geders v. United States, 425 U.S. 80, 86 (1976). We see no error in having the IRS present its case first, especially given that in this case, the IRS bears the burden of proving fraud by clear and convincing evidence. See
181
[*181] Houser v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 1995-330, 1995 WL 432633, at *18 (requiring IRS to present case first in fraud case).
Furthermore, Pinckney did not raise this issue of order of proof until the end of the fourth day of trial at the end of the IRS’s case-inchief. Pinckney’s complaint was thus unseasonable.
Furthermore, Pinckney was not prejudiced by having to present his case last. Pinckney asserts that the reason the Court should have had Pinckney present his case first is so that the IRS could have “an opportunity to Rebut.” It is inappropriate to grant Pinckney relief for an error that he says prejudiced the opposing party.
B. The Court did not err in not granting a continuance for Pinckney to attempt again to serve a subpoena on Myers.
Pinckney contends that the Court erred by denying his motion for a continuance to give him a chance to attempt to serve a trial subpoena on Myers.
A witness who is not served by a subpoena is not required to appear to testify. Mei Productions v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 2020-11, at *20 n.14.
Pinckney could have served Myers with a trial subpoena as early as February 12, 2021, the date we notified the parties that the trial would occur at a Trial Session to begin January 24, 2022. Pinckney did not attempt to serve the subpoena on Myers at any time before trial. On the second day of trial, at Pinckney’s request, the Court agreed to shorten the third trial day to free up Pinckney’s time so he could try to serve the subpoena. On the third day of trial, Pinckney tried and failed to serve Myers with a subpoena. On the fifth day of trial, the Court refused Pinckney’s request for a continuance to the next week to allow him time to attempt again to have the subpoena served. Ultimately, the IRS called Myers on the fifth day of trial as a rebuttal witness and Pinckney was allowed to ask Myers cross examination questions about Pinckney’s theory that the examination was a disguised criminal investigation.
The denial of the continuance was not an error. Pinckney was responsible for serving the subpoena on Myers. See Gillan v. Commissioner, T.C. Memo. 1988-321, 1988 Tax Ct. Memo LEXIS 349, at *6–7. He did not attempt to serve the subpoena on Myers until the third day of trial.
182
[*182] Furthermore, Pinckney was not prejudiced by his failure to serve the subpoena on Myers. Myers testified anyway, and Pinckney was allowed to ask questions of Myers related to his theory that the examination was a disguised criminal investigation. Thus, had the Court granted the continuance, and had Pinckney used the additional time to successfully serve the subpoena on Myers, and had Myers then testified as Pinckney’s witness, Pinckney would have been no better off.
C. Pinckney’s argument that the Court made adverse inferences from Pinckney’s refusal to testify, and that the Court erred in allegedly making such inferences, lacks merit.
Pinckney contends that the Court should not draw inferences against him from his refusals to testify.
In several instances during his testimony, Pinckney refused to answer the questions posed by IRS counsel, even after being ordered to by the Court. The IRS asked that the Court infer that the answers that Pinckney would have given would be adverse to Pinckney. However, the Court did not state during the trial that it would accede to the IRS’s request. In composing our findings of fact, we have not relied on adverse inferences. Furthermore, none of our findings of fact would be different had we made adverse inferences. Therefore, we need not resolve whether it is appropriate for the Court to accede to the IRS’s request to make adverse inferences.
D. Pinckney’s argument that the Court refused to allow Pinckney to cross-examine witnesses, and that the Court erred in allegedly so refusing, lacks merit.
Pinckney contends the Court erred by barring him from cross examining witnesses.
The IRS requested that the Court bar Pinckney from cross examining witnesses about those matters about which he had refused to testify. After the IRS made this request, the Court allowed Pinckney to cross examine witnesses called by the IRS. Therefore, the Court did not in fact bar Pinckney from cross-examining witnesses. So, even if it would have been an error for the Court to bar Pinckney from cross-examining witnesses, the Court did not commit such an error.
Decision will be entered under Rule 155.
